My own first version of this note listed the five fields as `link-credit`, `delivery-count`, `available`, `drain` and `echo`: **a count verified and a set inferred from memory**, which is the family above with a new shape — the extraction gave the number five and I supplied the members. The implementer checked the note against the ledger before acting on it and found the error, which is the only reason it did not reach a corpus.

The law itself is two ownership sentences that no keyword scan can see, which is why the ledger's phrase list was extended to catch them: `link-credit` says *"Only the receiver endpoint can independently set this value. The sender endpoint sets this to the last known value seen from the receiver"*, and `available` is its mirror for the sender. The link section states three more instances — *"Only the receiver can independently choose a value for this field"*, *"Only the sender can independently modify this field"*, *"Only the receiver can independently modify this field"* — so an endpoint never invents the partner's quantity, it echoes the last value it was sent. **That is the credit-conservation law in the artifact's own words, and it is now implemented and pinned rather than merely stated**: the session state holds this endpoint's own `deliveryCount`/`credit` and the partner's last reported `peerCount`/`peerCredit`, three checks are in the corpus (`delivery-count.2` for the sender's own current count, `delivery-count.3` for the peer's flow carrying exactly the count the view holds, and the ownership refusal for a sender's flow whose `link-credit` differs from the credit this receiver last sent) — and adding the ownership check *refused one of the implementer's own setup steps*, which had the sender granting credit it had no right to. A rule that catches its author's setup error is the evidence that it bites.

**The statement exists as of now, and it is statement-only on purpose.** `lean/Contracts/SessionCredit.lean` carries `SenderCreditInvariant`: in a state where this endpoint attached as the link's sender and a flow state exists, that state's credit is exactly `Position.creditFor` of the peer's last reported pair against this endpoint's own delivery-count — stated through the specification's own constructor so the two cannot drift apart. Its documentation records what discharges it (initialisation at `attach`, where the truncated right side is zero by the ownership sentence; preservation per transition, where a received flow *sets* the equation by construction and a transfer *preserves* it because both sides move by one, with the credit gate stopping the truncated subtraction from wrapping), records the three guards as corpus scenarios rather than restating them, records that the receiver's side has no state law and why, and records the status plainly: **true by construction rather than proved**, because the preservation lemma does not exist yet. The acceptance theorem lands in the file when it does — a contract that states a law and says which obligation discharges it is worth more than one that quietly assumes the law holds.

**The strengthened clause forced a lemma's statement to name its reachable states — and the first reading of that finding, by the implementer and then by me in this plan, was wrong.** With the guard tree reduced, one branch's lemma failed under the law: the partner-sender branch, recording the partner's count while leaving the position alone where there is none, so that a session in `position = none` with `peerCount = initialCount` violates the absence clause. That was read as a defect in the layer, and the plan said so. **It is not, and the verification is what settles it**: the branch records the partner's count *only* when this endpoint's role is `receiver`, and that role is written in exactly two places — our own `attach`, whose match on the role always yields a position, and `detach`, which clears the role and the position together. So role-receiver implies a position exists, the branch is unreachable with no position, and the law holds in every reachable state. **The law is fine, the layer is right, and the defect was the lemma's statement** — stated over an arbitrary session, which is stronger than the branch can see: over an arbitrary session the record does violate the clause, and it is the branch's own guard that makes that unreachable. The lemma now carries those restrictions as hypotheses, which is where they belong.

So the escalated test's answer has **three** categories rather than two: the law is too weak; the implementation is wrong; or the statement is stronger than its subject. A proof obligation stronger than the code's behaviour is evidence about the *statement*, not about the code — and the reflex to read it the other way is what both the implementer and this plan did before checking. The absence clause still earned its place twice over: it made the induction possible, and it forced a statement to say which states it is about, which it could not have done while the equation was vacuous wherever `position` was `none`.

**That vacuity finding and this one are a pair, and the pair is the general form.** A statement can fail to match its subject's reachable set in either direction: *vacuous where the work is*, so it cannot fail where it matters; or *universal where only reachable states are at issue*, so it fails where nothing is. Both were caught by attempting a proof rather than by reading a statement, and the remedy is one sentence in both cases — state the property over the states the subject actually reaches.

**The family's shapes and the corrections this plan has taken are one rule, and the implementer supplied the unification.** A claim's source must be the thing the claim is about: for a claim about the code, the code; for a claim about a run, the run's own row count; for a claim about a binary, its build's job count; for a claim about the artifact, the artifact. Five instances from a single session, each reading *something adjacent* to the subject and treating it as the subject:

* a handle-less-flow field list derived from an extraction's count, with the members supplied from memory;
* a "fifth defect" concluded from a failing statement rather than a failing implementation;
* a clause routed by hand and then doubted without opening the file;
* a probe's silence read as its outcome, where the file had been rejected;
* a zero-failure corpus standing behind a build that had failed under `/dev/null`, so the binary was never rebuilt.

**The operational forms are receipts**, and that is why the rule is checkable rather than merely prudent: a run reports its extent, a build reports its job count, a contract states a prediction rather than a record, and a check states what it is checking. Every instrument in this repository that has been trusted this session has produced one of those four; the two that lied did so by omitting it.

**The Lean-level lessons are four now, and the newest is about the *form* of a hypothesis rather than its content.** A branch hypothesis written `s.position.isSome` blocked `cases hstep` with a dependent-elimination failure — `failed to solve equation true = match s.position with | some val => true | none => false` — because a proposition that mentions a field makes the elimination dependent on that field, the successor having been built from it. Restating the identical fact as `s.position = some p` lets the same proof through unchanged. Both forms say there is a position; only one is usable where the successor was built from the field, and the difference is invisible until a proof is attempted. The rule for the next person stating a branch hypothesis: **state facts about values rather than propositions that mention fields.** Recorded with the other three, since they are one class of knowledge a prover here needs — `cases hb : e` inside a goal that mentions `e` leaves `hb` unusable exactly where the conclusion needs it; splitting a match needs `split at h` rather than `cases` on the discriminant; and a guard wrapper like `refuseUnless` needs its own `unfold` before the tree will reduce at all.

**And the third category landed rather than being described.** The escalation was withdrawn because the code is right, the lemma now exists, and the only thing that ever changed was how the statement names the states its subject reaches — three named branch lemmas stand where one failing statement did, and the file was green at every point in between, which is precisely what landing a residue per branch is for.

**The reduction's residue is enumeration rather than search, and that is a fact about the code rather than about the tactic.** With the guard tree unfolded, `cases hstep` substitutes each surviving branch's record — and in two branches closes the goal outright because the branch's left side is a refusal. A uniform discharge over the nine fails, because one branch's record matches neither content lemma and `simp` makes no progress on it: the nine discharges each read their own record and instantiate `fresh_link_sender_credit` where the branch assigns the establishing four, or `frame_sender_credit` at the identity everywhere else. **A uniform tactic failing over nine different records is not the law being too weak** — the escalation answer stands, every target is the invariant at a record the branch builds — and the practical consequence is a discipline worth keeping: land such a residue as **per-branch lemmas** rather than one long body, since each is provable alone as pure record plumbing, so every stretch of work ends with a compiling tree and a named branch, instead of a revert and a report. **And the goal list is written down so the next sitting does not rediscover it**: with the guard tree unfolded and the same `split`/`simp` loop, `attachLink` leaves **nine** goals — the two role reads, five under the handle read and its two guards, and one bare `isFalse`. Three are landed as lemmas; six remain, and they are the refusals, which close inline inside `cases hstep` because their left side is an `.error`, and the unchanged-session ones, which are the framing lemma at four reflexivities. **The refusal branches are deliberately not named as lemmas**, on the argument that their statements would spell out long refusal expressions for no content — the per-branch landing rule applied where branches carry content and declined where they only close a shape, which is the right reading of it.**The reduction needs one word more than it looks like, and the escalation rule answered no.** `unfold attachLink at hstep` leaves the guard tree inert: `refuseUnless` is itself a wrapper around an `if`, so the guards never surface to the `split` loop. `unfold attachLink refuseUnless at hstep` reduces the whole tree cleanly, with no stuck goals and no unsolved obligations from the guards — a Lean-level lesson of the same kind as the ones the frame proofs recorded, and one worth having in writing because it looks like it should work without the second name. **And the widening's corpus effect is predicted before the patch, with its boundary named.** The predicate change moves a *received* refusal's landing state to `DISCARDING` for every state that can still receive **(corrected below: *received* was too broad — a wire-level refusal takes no placement at all, and only the placement path is widened)**, so the affected vectors are exactly those whose refusal arrives in a receiving state that is not `MAPPED` — which in the corpus as it stands is one step, `exchange-session-send-end-in-begin-sent`'s second, expecting `END_SENT` today and `DISCARDING` after. Every other received refusal arrives while the session is `MAPPED`, where the predicate already fires. **Writing that down before the patch is what makes the difference visible**: if the corpus moves anywhere else when the widening lands, it is a state nobody has thought about rather than noise, and an effect predicted in advance is the only form in which that distinction survives. The same patch adds a vector whose refusal arrives in `END_SENT` for a reason *other* than the channel map — an unattached handle is the cheapest — so the new predicate is tested by a *rule* rather than by the state's maps: without it the channel map answers first and the predicate is shadowed, which is the shadowed-expectation finding applied before the vector is written rather than after it fails.**And no stronger statement is needed for `attachLink`, which is the answer the rule was built to get.** Every surviving branch's target is `SenderCreditState` at the record that branch builds; the establishing branch sets `role := some sender`, `position := some ⟨initialCount, 0⟩`, `peerCount := 0` and `peerCredit := 0`, which are the content lemma's four hypotheses verbatim, and the unchanged branches are the framing lemma at the identity. So what remains is per-branch record plumbing rather than mathematics. **That the branch shapes land exactly on the lemmas written before the reduction is the sign the decomposition was right**: the law's five obligations — initialisation, the framing case, and one per operation that moves the quantities — turned out to be the code's own branch structure, which is what a law shaped by the artifact rather than by the implementation is supposed to give.

**The hand-written probe is now a repository instrument**: `scripts/probe-link-flow-field-without-handle.ndjson`, two steps with the thirty-one octets spelled out, its own note saying what it is, how to run it, and that mutating the coupling and re-running it tests the rule without regenerating anything. It passes in *both* artefacts — the reference independently implements the same coupling — and it is the one instrument here whose result depends on neither the generator, the step builder, the vector vocabulary nor the corpus. It exists because the corpus's own vector could not be trusted to test the rule it named, so it is the standing answer to that class of question rather than a one-off.

**The preservation work found four behavioural defects in the layer, and the corpus had not.** A transfer charged credit per *frame* rather than per *message*, so a multi-transfer delivery spent it once per transfer and a continuation was refused at exhausted credit although its delivery was already counted. A `detach` released the link but left the credit and the partner's count behind, so a re-attach reset the equation's left side against a stale right one. A received `attach` set this endpoint's view of the partner's count when the partner was not the link's sender, letting a both-senders exchange move a quantity its owner had never announced. And that same `attach` reset the position of a link already established. **All four are fixed, one is pinned by a vector** (`exchange-link-credit-counts-messages-not-frames`: one delivery of credit across three transfers, with the exhaustion shown on the delivery that begins a second message) — and the other three are recorded here as *fixed but unpinned*, because a fix without a vector can regress silently and the corpus would then imply a coverage it does not have. Pinning them is the next task, before the three missing preservation lemmas.

That four defects of this kind surfaced from *stating an invariant* rather than from running frames is the clearest evidence yet of what the two instruments do differently: a corpus tests the sequences it names, and an invariant tests the states the operations reach, including the ones nobody thought to sequence. Every fix is the same ownership sentence applied at a site — the partner's quantities belong to the partner's role, and a link that is established or released has agreed nothing — which is a sign the sentence was worth formalising rather than paraphrasing in a comment.

**Two of those defects cannot be pinned by a vector at all, and the reason is stronger than the argument above.** `peerCount` and `peerCredit` have no *reader* in either layer: grepping both session modules, the names occur only in their field declarations, in the assignments that write them, and once as a fallback inside one of those assignments — no guard, no branch condition, no comparison and no outcome depends on either. So a stale credit and a role-blind view update are not observable as a step's verdict, and no vector count can reach them, because there is nothing to observe. The invariant is not merely the instrument that happened to find these two; it is the *only* instrument that can, and the strengthened clause `position = none → peerCount = 0 ∧ peerCredit = 0` is exactly what carries them. **So the formal side is not testing states the corpus forgot to sequence — it is testing state the corpus cannot reach by construction.** That is a sharper claim than the one this plan made when the first four defects surfaced, and it is the strongest available answer to what the proofs are for.

**The offered remedy is declined, and the reason matters more than the decline.** One could make the quantities observable by refusing a flow that reports a `delivery-count` this endpoint never saw. That is a *new rule* rather than a pin: the artifact states that a sender's flow MUST carry its current count, not that a receiver must police a count it has no independent knowledge of, and inventing a refusal so that a test can see a field is designing the protocol around the instrument — the same error as pinning a vector to the implementation's behaviour, with the arrow reversed. The fields stay unread and the invariant stays their reader. Worth stating plainly what they are for, since it is not obvious from the code: the equation is precisely what makes reading them unnecessary — the sender's credit is *defined* as their combination with its own count, so the state's meaning is carried by the law rather than by a branch.

**And a discipline point the third pin raised by being honest about itself.** That pin — `exchange-link-attach-keeps-the-credit`, the partner granting credit and a second `attach` naming an unseen handle, with the transfer afterwards admitted on the granted credit — was written *after* the fix, so it has never been observed failing. A pin that has not been seen to fail is a test whose bite is inferred rather than witnessed, which is the family above in its mildest form. The remedy is cheap and worth making a rule: revert the fix, watch the vector fail, restore the fix. The corpus is a set of claims about the implementation, and a claim about the *previous* implementation is the one that proves the claim has content.

**Making a pin witnessed is mutation testing, and it found more than the pin was written for.** Reverting the attach guard failed exactly one vector and nothing else, which is the pin being the fix's bite and nothing more. Reverting the credit accounting failed *three*: the two continuation steps the author had predicted, and a vector in another family — `exchange-link-credit-granted-and-spent` step 5 — admitted where it expects a refusal. That third failure is the same defect from the other end and nobody had reasoned it: with the pre-fix condition, a delivery whose single transfer leaves `more` unset never incremented the delivery-count at all, so the credit it should have spent was still there and a second delivery was admitted free. **So the fix was two-sided — over-charging a multi-transfer delivery and never charging a single-transfer one — and the corpus now witnesses both.** The general lesson is worth keeping: a revert performed to witness a pin *is* a mutation test, and a mutant killed by a vector outside its expected family is either a coupled rule or a missing isolation, both of which are findings rather than noise.

**An unwitnessed pin sourced from the artifact is materially safer than an unwitnessed pin copied from behaviour, and the difference is worth stating precisely.** Of the link family, the handle-in-use and handle-max refusals, the credit grant and echo, the fragmentation family, the settled cases, the detach exception and the disposition direction were each written *with* the rule they state, so their bite is by construction and it would be dishonest to call it observed. What separates them from the family this plan tracks is their **source rather than their bite**: they were written from the artifact's clauses and the code was then made to satisfy them, which is why landing the link machine made nineteen of them fail at once rather than making all of them pass. A pin copied from behaviour agrees with the code whatever the code does; a pin stating a rule the code was written to satisfy cannot agree by construction. The one gap this leaves is a *shared misreading* — vector and implementation reading the same clause the same wrong way — and that is what the register, the session sweep's independently derived model and the third-party admission of §12 exist to catch.

**The unwitnessed list is the adequacy workstream's opening target**, and its first three are cheap: the attach and handshake refusals, one revert each, with the rule that a revert may only fail its own family's vectors and anything outside is a finding rather than an inconvenience.

**A surviving mutant means the vector is not testing the rule it names — and that is the isolation check, not a nuisance.** The third witness left the corpus green with its rule disabled, and the reason was in the vector: it sent a handle-less `flow` *without* `next-incoming-id`, so the session's own mandatory-field rule refused it first, with the same class and condition, and the coupling rule never got a look. Two rules in one layer able to refuse the same frame with the same observable is a vector that cannot distinguish them, and no amount of reading the corpus would have shown it — the mutation showed it, because a rule that cannot be mutated out from under a vector is a rule the vector is not exercising. So the mutation is the isolation check as well as the witness: **a vector's refusal must be attributable to exactly one rule, and the only way to know is to disable that rule and watch the vector fail.** Two of the three probes did exactly that and failed one vector each, nothing outside their family; the third failed the check and produced a vector defect worth more than the witness would have been.

**The vector was then rebuilt to be isolating on purpose** — carrying `next-incoming-id` and using `available`, which nothing else in the layer reads, precisely so that the coupling is the only rule that could refuse it — and the mutation *still* left the corpus green, which with the handle-less branch entered and the refusal not firing is the shape of a fourth defect: a guard whose branch is reached and whose refusal does not fire for the input it was written for. That question is open, with the instrument to settle it named rather than guessed at.



Two things the S4 contract will therefore **not** claim, each for a stated reason: `available` is not tracked, because it is advisory and the model holds no sender-side quantity for a receiver to echo, and `drain`'s direction-dependent meaning (actual mode sender to receiver, desired mode receiver to sender) is not modelled because nothing in the slice reads it. Where a contract's subject is not modelled, the contract says so rather than approximating it.

**The flow sibling is implemented and pinned, and my doubt about it was a claim sourced from the wrong place.** `Spec/Session.lean:692` calls `linkHandleOf session outbound "flow" body` and `Ref/Session.lean:508` calls `readHandle endpoint outbound "flow" body`, so a flow naming an unattached handle is refused with `amqp:session:unattached-handle` in both layers, pinned by `exchange-link-flow-unattached-handle` with a send refusal leaving `MAPPED` and a receive refusal leaving `DISCARDING`. **Three of this plan's claims have now been corrected by the implementer, and the pattern is one thing**: the five handle-less-flow fields came from an extraction's count with the members supplied from memory; the "fifth defect" came from reading a failing statement and concluding a failing implementation; and this came from doubting my own routing of a clause without opening the file. **Every one was a claim about the implementation sourced from something other than the implementation.** The rule this repository states for artifacts — the artifact is the source, the implementation is a suspect — has a mirror for claims about the code: the code is the source, and a count, a report or a recollection is the suspect.

**And a note about known state must state what is implemented, not what a disagreement would mean.** I warned the session sweep that the flow sibling might be missing and that a split family would therefore be a known candidate. The warning was false, and it was dangerous in the direction that matters: the sweep's model is mechanical and cannot be bent, but the *report* is judgment, so handing the author an interpretation in advance converts a disagreement into an expected outcome. Facts about what is implemented prevent false alarms; interpretations pre-empt findings; and the checker's report is where the judgment has to happen.

**The scope pair stands as recorded**: nothing about `available` and `drain` is modelled beyond their field clauses — no sender-side quantity is tracked for a receiver to echo, and `drain`'s direction-dependent meaning is unmodelled because nothing in the slice reads it — so a sweep probe of either admits rather than failing, and that admission is the correct answer rather than a gap.

**The first of the three sweep fixes is now witnessed, and it is the twenty-cell family — with the isolation evidence a mutation needs.** With the reference's channel maps reverted, the corpus reports **exactly and only the two new vectors, both of their steps each**, four failures in all, while the specification stays green; restored, 266 step verdicts pass in both artefacts. A mutant killed by precisely the vectors written for it is what the isolation rule asks for, and it is the second confirmed instance after the handle probes. The instrument that made the vectors writable was cleared first: the earlier start-state failure was the probe's own — two steps copied from another vector without fixing their send and receive roles, so a send was fed to `END_SENT`'s incoming side — and with the directions right, `END_SENT` receives and stays, `END_RCVD` sends and stays, and each refuses the other direction with "channel 1 is not mapped to this session, whose incoming channel is none", which is `sessions.10`'s own subject stated back as a refusal.

**And the start-state requirement is refined by its first implementation.** The corpus now starts at eleven states — the connection's seven plus `session:{MAPPED, UNMAPPED, END_SENT, END_RCVD}` — and the remaining three are **unused rather than unstartable**: `atState` handles all seven session names including `BEGIN_SENT`, `BEGIN_RCVD` and `DISCARDING`, so every `session:` state the artifact names is startable on the path those two vectors proved, and what a per-state listing should report is *usedness*, with "unused" as the reason and "unstartable" reserved for a state the path genuinely cannot enter. The distinction is worth keeping: one is a coverage fact about the corpus, the other a defect in the instrument, and conflating them would hide the second inside the first.

**Three suspicions in a row have now been cleared with the instrument as the suspect** — the probe's roles, the rejected one-step file, the build behind `/dev/null` — and the layer was right in each case, while the same discipline is what let the sweep's real findings stand. The rule is not that the instrument is usually guilty; it is that the instrument is checked *first*, so that a layer finding arrives with an instrument that cannot be blamed for it.

**A corpus that reaches states by stepping cannot see what a state's entry assignment gives it — and that is why twenty cells were invisible.** The two positive vectors written for the swapped channel maps passed in *both* artefacts **with the maps reverted**, and the reason is a fact about the state rather than about the vectors: a state reached by a step keeps the channel maps of the state it came from, so an `END_SENT` reached from `MAPPED` still carries both maps and admits everything the artifact permits. Only a vector that *starts* at `END_SENT` or `END_RCVD` can see the assignment the artifact gives them. The general form is worth stating because it bounds every sequence corpus in this repository: **the corpus measures transitions, and a state's own fields are visible only at entry** — so every field `atState` assigns is outside the reach of a sequence corpus by construction, and inside the sweep's reach because its start states come from the artifact's table rather than from what a generator happens to emit.

**And the start-state gap is now measured rather than suspected.** The corpus's start states are exactly `connection:{START, HDR_SENT, HDR_EXCH, OPEN_SENT, OPENED, DISCARDING, END}` and `session:{MAPPED, UNMAPPED}` — nine — with `session:END_SENT` and `session:END_RCVD` absent. That is the same currency as finding 1's thirty-eight cells, and it turns an observation into a corpus-completeness requirement: **every state the artifact names must be startable in the corpus, or the reason it cannot be must be recorded.** The second clause matters because a start state that exists but does not behave as `atState` intends is an instrument question first and a layer question second — which is the order the implementer applied when the rewritten vectors failed with the peer reported as still in `MAPPED`, and declined to call it a finding about the layer before looking at its own path.

**The three fixes restated in that light**: none is witnessed, and now with a precise reason rather than a general one. The channel-map fix has **no step-reachable route at all**, since only a start state carries its assigned maps, so its witness waits on the start-state path being understood; the role-direction condition is step-reachable, so its vector is writable and its revert will be a real witness. The two rewritten vectors were removed rather than left failing, and the corpus is green again at 65 vectors / 262 step verdicts in both artefacts.

**A correction pass is not a rule, and the condition conventions regrew at the next site written.** The 28-cell disagreement was not a stale layer — which was my reading, and wrong — but a *newer* site: the transfer role-direction rule, written after the correction pass and wrong in **both** layers, so the pass fixed every site it visited and nothing mechanical told the author of the next one which condition a role refusal carries. **This is a new shape for the plan's collection**: not a check that agrees with what it checks, but a *convention that decays wherever it is not mechanically enforced*. It is also the strongest argument yet for the sweep, whose cells ask what condition *every* refusal carries rather than what a named vector asserts; both layers name `amqp:illegal-state` at that site now.

**And the twenty behavioural cells were two swapped lines.** `Ref/Session.lean`'s `Endpoint.atState` gave `endSent` an outgoing channel and `endRcvd` an incoming one, against the artifact's assignment — `END_SENT` has an incoming entry and no outgoing number, `END_RCVD` the reverse — while the specification had both right. Twenty cells hung on a transposition, now fixed, and the diagnosis located it to the function.

**The exit property ran, and its single failure is the six-cell finding reached by a second instrument.** Breadth-first over each machine's own diagram: all seven session states are reachable by admissions (UNMAPPED in 0 steps, MAPPED in 2, DISCARDING in 3) and every one has a mandated exit; thirteen of the fourteen connection states are reachable by admissions, DISCARDING only by a refusal, and **END is terminal rather than a dead end** — the diagram draws no edge out of it, which is a distinction worth keeping, since a state the artifact names as an end is not a state the specification forgot to leave. On the layers' side, nineteen of the twenty mandated exits are admitted by both artefacts. **The twentieth is `END_RCVD`'s**: picture 30 draws `S:END` from `END_RCVD` to `UNMAPPED`, and both artefacts refuse it — "channel 1 is not this session's outgoing channel, none", with `amqp:connection:framing-error` — which is the label-versus-map defect, found from the *reachability walk* rather than from the permission table.

**A shared-file collision, caught before anyone acted on the state it disturbed.** V4's first mutant was planted in `Spec/Session.lean:750` while the same file was being edited for the sweep's fixes, and the layer work witnesses by snapshotting that file with `cp` and restoring — so **a restore reinstates whatever was in the file when the snapshot was taken, which may be a sibling's experiment**, and it did: the layer work's own restore handed back a copy that contained the mutant. The collision was found by the layer work, flagged instead of acted on, and the file left exactly as it stood rather than restored over a third time; the mutant was then reverted by *patch*, the `cp` copy deleted so it cannot be misused by anyone including its author, and the differential verified green. Two rules came out of it, both now in force: **a mutation run is a quiet window** — the files it depends on, and the corpus it is measured by, have one owner while a mutant is planted, the window is announced with its start and end, and the record carries the diff — and **a snapshot restore is not a safe mutation protocol in a shared tree**, since mutating by patch at least makes the file's content a function of the mutant rather than of whoever wrote last.

**And the void run had a cause, which named the family's fourth instance: a shadowed expectation.** The failure the mutant produced was `exchange-session-transfer-one-data-section#3` refused with `amqp:illegal-state` where the vector names `amqp:session:unattached-handle` — and `amqp:illegal-state` is the *role* rule, "a transfer is the sender's frame", answering before the handle rule is reached, because the vector sends a transfer on a session with no link and the role can only be `none`. **The vector names one rule and another answers it**, so dropping the rule it names changes nothing and the mutation is undeathable however often it is run. That is the isolation property violated from the other side: isolation asks that exactly one rule *can* refuse the frame, and shadowing is a different rule *doing* it. The prescription is the shape the flow's own vector uses — attach a link, satisfy every other rule the layer reads, violate exactly one field — and one mutant thus produced two corpus gaps: the transfer vector needs rewriting into that shape, and `detach` has no such vector at all. The same read gave the suite a shape rule: `linkHandleOf` is shared by flow, transfer and detach, so a mutant of it is only killed completely by a vector *per performative*.

**Ruling on the descriptions, because the question the vector exposed is the artifact's and not the code's.** Picture 30 draws `BEGIN_SENT --S:END--> END_SENT`, while `BEGIN_SENT`'s description says it is assigned an outgoing channel with no incoming entry and `END_SENT`'s says it has an incoming entry and no outgoing assignment. **The descriptions mix two kinds of statement: what the transition does to a channel, and what the route produced.** The first clause of each is the machine's guarantee — entering `END_SENT` releases the outgoing channel, entering `END_RCVD` releases the incoming one — and the second is a characterisation of the ordinary route (`MAPPED --S:END--> END_SENT` and its mirror), where the peer's begin has already mapped the other side. So the label is the *phase*, not a claim about which channels exist, and the machines are right: the label moves, the outgoing channel is released, and the incoming entry is whatever the route left. A session that never received a begin has none, and the next received frame is then refused by the channel map with `amqp:connection:framing-error` — which is correct, because an unmapped incoming channel cannot carry a frame. The reading is registered; **neither artefact's behaviour changes for it and no description is edited**, since the descriptions are the artifact's and the reading is ours to record rather than to rewrite.

**The narrowing is required by two MUSTs, verified verbatim rather than quoted.** `sessions.5` ("When a session is unable to process input, it MUST indicate this by issuing an END with an appropriate «error»…") and `sessions.6` ("It MUST then proceed to discard all incoming frames from the remote endpoint until receiving the remote endpoint's corresponding «end» frame") make the discarding phase unconditional once a session error fires, so a placement conditioned on `state == .mapped` satisfies it from one state only — and `END_SENT` can receive. The predicate the artifact implies is **"this state can still receive"**, derived from `sessions.6`'s own words, with `sessions.12` supplying the phase's content ("any incoming frames on the session MUST be silently discarded until the peer's «end» frame is received"). That is the one change found today that the artifact *requires* rather than licenses, and it lands with its `Ref` twin or not at all: half a rule would appear in the differential as a disagreement that is not one.

**And the collision destroyed a fix, which the caveat had predicted.** The spec-side `BEGIN_SENT` branch was landed and then lost to a snapshot restore. The chain is worth keeping in full, because every link is a different instrument: the layer work's own warning that a restore "may have reinstated a version without it, at a moment you did not choose"; the regression instrument's four new divergences, measured on the same day it was built, naming `probe-begin-sent-send-end` and `probe-begin-sent-end-error`; and a read of both functions — `Ref/Session.lean:733` has `| .beginSent => { endpoint with state := .endSent, outgoing := none }` with a comment stating the ruling, while `Spec/Session.lean:969` falls into `| other` and leaves the label. So `ref` is the reference of record for that transition until the spec re-lands the line with the window's close, and its comment already says what the ruling says. **And the hazard's actual product is now known, which is narrower than either of the possibilities its author named when flagging it**: not an experiment reinstated and not a mutation silently left in place, but **a landed fix removed** — the spec's `BEGIN_SENT` branch was written, built, reported and then taken out by a restore that reinstated an earlier copy of the file. A snapshot protocol in a shared tree fails by *losing* work as readily as by contaminating it, and losing work is invisible in exactly the way the other direction is not: nothing in the tree then says a line is missing, and only an instrument that had run *before* the change could notice. That is the strongest form of the case for mutating by patch with the diff as the record, and it is why the branch is queued as a patch to apply rather than as a state to restore.**V4's first three mutants each confirmed the tier declared for them before the run.** A `sorry` in `Spec/Value.lean` builds cleanly, with Lean's warning and no gate, and fails `s1_proof_integrity.sh` with file and line across 36 scanned modules — the gate shown load-bearing on the specification itself. A descriptor collision in the generated tables also builds cleanly, at 34 jobs, "because the compiler cannot see it, which is the point", and dies at the table-currency contract instead: the confirmation, from the other side, that `lean/Generated` is outside the proof-integrity scan precisely because the generators' byte-identity contract is what checks it. And the dropped handle rule — invisible to the differential, which compared **68,120 vectors and reported identical verdicts from both artefacts** — was killed by the sweep as 29 divergences, 21 of them the mutant's signature. **That is the complementarity demonstrated in a single experiment rather than argued**: the differential compares two artefacts' answers, and a rule whose refusal is produced by something else on both sides is invisible to it, while an instrument whose expectation table comes from the clauses is not.

**The session machine's own statement was unreviewed, and is now disposed.** `picture.30` — the state diagram both artefacts implement and that the sweep's model equals — carried `looks_normative: false` and no disposition, because the flag's criterion is grammar and normative keywords and a graph has neither. It is disposed anyway, as `formalized:SpecAMQP.Spec.Session.atState`, with a note naming the `Session.after*` family as the edges and recording why the exclusion had to be a decision rather than an artifact of the flag. **That is the same shape as the sweep's misread diagnostic and the corpus that encoded the implementation's error, one medium further out**: a criterion keyed on text, applied to a class whose meaning is structural.

**And three misreadings today, one per agent, every one caught by another.** A field list inferred from an extraction's count; a rendered diagnostic read for a field it never prints; a comment quoted from memory of a grep window. Each was a claim at one remove from its subject, and each was corrected by whoever held the subject — which is the arrangement working.**One case is not settled by that reading and is routed back as a narrow question.** A session error raised in `END_SENT` clears the incoming channel while the label stays `END_SENT` — inconsistent in the *other* direction, because a session error ends the session and the label should reach the discarding phase. That is a real question about the machine rather than about the reading.That is the first time two of this programme's instruments have independently reached the same layer defect, and it makes the finding much harder to be an artifact of either. A permission sweep asks what a state permits; a reachability walk asks what leads out of it; both arrive at a state whose description assigns a channel its map no longer has. It is also the only mandated exit in either machine that does not hold, so the layer's fix closes the exit property as well as the six cells.

**And V4 has started with its expectations declared before the run.** Mutant 1 is a dropped MUST check — `linkHandleOf` for `transfer` no-opped in `Spec/Session.lean:750` — with the differential corpus named as the expected catcher, since the flipped unattached-handle vectors pin that refusal, and the sweep's unattached-handle family as the second tier, where a family going from 21/21 refused to a split is the SCATTER flag doing what it was built for. The record carries the build and its job count, the expected diagnostic, what was seen, the revert and the diff. **And a sixth mutant has been added for a *tier* rather than a rule: a proof replaced by `sorry`, which the proof-integrity gate should catch** — if it does not, that is a finding about the gate, which is a better thing for a mutant to find than a missing check in a layer.

**Finding 1 is withdrawn: the reading was of a rendering that omits the condition, not of a refusal without one.** The sweep's probes pinned a condition but no reason class, the runner compares the class first, and its rendering for a class mismatch — "refused as illegalState, and the vector names : …" — prints the vector's own pin and never the peer's condition; the reader took the first `amqp:` name in the line, found none, and recorded "absent". The tell is in the same line: the vector's pin rendered empty. **The independent proof is the corpus's own vector for the same rule**, which pins both and passes — `condition: amqp:connection:framing-error, reason: illegalState` for a frame on a channel that is not the session's — so the implementer's reading stands, and the 101 cells whose raw text could then be read show the other half: a *state* refusal names `amqp:illegal-state` in both layers, a condition present and a different one from the channel case. Both were invisible for the same reason, and it generalises the family once more: **a diagnostic that prints only what differs cannot be read for the absence of the same field** — the fix belongs in the reader rather than in the finding, which is where it went.

**The re-run is clean and leaves seven deviations, six of which are a real finding.** 0 divergences between the artefacts, 0 predictions missed, 0 unobserved, 268 agreements. **Six cells show a state label that no longer matches the channel map its description assigns**: a sent end in `BEGIN_SENT` releases the outgoing channel while the label stays, and after the unattached-handle refusal in `END_SENT` the incoming channel is gone while the label stays — so the next frame is refused by the channel map rather than by anything the state says. The artifact assigns each state its maps, so a label persisting while its assignment is gone is an inconsistency between a state and its own description, and it is routed to the layer's owner.

**The seventh is the probe's own, and it is a confirmation rather than a defect**: a sent transfer needs the credit the receiver granted, and its probe granted none, so the link's credit answered with `limit` before the session's window — **the credit-conservation law seen from outside the layer proving it**, which is the kind of evidence a permission sweep can give a proof.

**And two instrument corrections of the same family went in with it**: the reader takes the outcome's state from the runner's own "but the peer is in X" rather than from the layer's message — which names the state it answered *from* — and treats a refusal the harness *accepted* as the condition having been compared and found equal, rather than re-reading text the harness prints only when it differs.**Three fixes landed, none witnessed, and the reason is the corpus's blind spot.** Each changes a cell no vector covers — the role refusal's condition, a receive in `END_SENT`, a send in `END_RCVD` — so the vectors come first and the reverts second, which is the order the witnessed-pin rule requires. The twenty behavioural cells want *positive* vectors and will fail against the old reference, so a real witness is available rather than a reasoned one. That the fixes land on uncovered cells is the same coverage gap the sweep's 38 cells measure, in the same currency.

**The slice's clause dispositions were audited, and the acceptance claim is not yet true.** Of 230 MUST-class clauses, 205 are undispositioned, and **35 of those fall inside the slice's own scope**: Part 0's conformance framework (11), the transport section's twelve, five on `begin`'s fields, the session section's four, and the connection section's three. They include the clauses this session's work turned on — `sessions.5`, `sessions.6` and `sessions.12`, whose widening is queued; the `session-flow-control` decrement and update rules, which *are* the window arithmetic the V1 invariants are about; `handle-max`'s two, which the corpus pins; and `remote-channel`'s pair, one of which the corpus covers and one of which it does not. So S3's acceptance item, "the slice's clauses are dispositioned and formalized", is a claim the ledger does not currently support — which is the coverage count doing exactly what it exists for. A read-only mapping pass is dispatched to name, for each of the 35, the declaration or vector that carries it or nothing at all, after which the dispositions are written with those names in them. **An undispositioned clause is not automatically an uncarried one**, and telling the two apart is what the mapping is for.  **V4 is complete, and its two survivors are the right result: suite defects rather than code defects, both located.** Four of the six mutants were killed by the tier declared for them before the run — a `sorry` in `Spec/Value.lean` by the proof-integrity gate across 36 scanned modules, a descriptor collision in the generated tables by the table-currency check at a clean 34-job build (the compiler cannot see it, which is the point), and a non-canonical `uint` writer by the differential, which is the hand-authored primitive corpus earning its place a second time. The two survivors were both *uncaught specification mutations*, which the plan's own rule says to report as suite defects rather than tolerate, and each came with the exact vector that would have caught it. **The quiet window has a second half, and the first request to re-open one is where it showed.** After V4 closed and the layer work's patch run began, the sweep asked to re-open the window for one mutant in `Spec/Connection.lean` — the channel bound widened by one, re-planted so that the record could say *killed by the new vector* rather than *survived*, since it has added the boundary vector the survivor was missing. The answer was wait, because **a mutant in any file an in-flight build reads corrupts the measurement**: the differential compiles the whole specification, `Connection.lean` is inside that closure, and a run measuring the tree and a mutant mutating it cannot occupy the same minute. The rule is therefore symmetric and now stated that way — **a mutation run is a quiet window, and a measurement run is one too; each waits for the other to finish.** Nothing was lost by waiting, since the probe is the deliverable either way, and what was avoided is a corpus report whose numbers came from a tree with a planted defect in it — a class of result this session has already discarded once. **The mirror rule earned itself within a minute, and the instance is a withdrawn run.** The exit-property re-run finished against the pre-patch tree and showed `reach-end_rcvd` refused as expected *and* `reach-end_sent` newly refused — which its author diagnosed as the patch mid-flight rather than a finding, since `END_SENT`'s exit passed in the baseline, and withdrew instead of reporting. That is the rule working exactly as written, one step over from mutation: a measurement taken during someone else's *change* is a number about a tree nobody chose, whether the change is a planted mutant or a half-landed patch. **And the patch landed green with the boundary failing in the useful way: one prediction over-general, one placement site missed.** Both artefacts at 278 step verdicts and zero failures, `s1_differential` and `s1_ref_vectors` passing. Two steps moved where one was predicted. **The first was an over-general prediction rather than a defect**: a wire-level channel refusal is raised *outside* the placement path and leaves the state where it is — which the register's `unmapped-channel-refusal` reading already records, so the prediction contradicted a reading that was sitting there to be consulted, and the expectation went back to `END_SENT`. **The second was a real miss, and the vector built to isolate the predicate is what found it**: the specification has *two* placement sites, `placed` for the session's own refusals and `place` for the ones the link raises, and only the first was widened — so a link refusal in `END_SENT` still left `END_SENT` and the isolating vector failed on its first run. It would otherwise have shipped as a predicate that appeared to work, because every other refusal in the corpus arrives while the session is `MAPPED`, where the old and new predicates agree. That is the shadowing argument paying out in the other direction: a vector built so the predicate would be tested by a *rule* rather than by the maps turned out to be the only test of one of the two sites at all. **And the shadowed bound resolved to a one-sided gap with the receiving half already right.** A probe eight steps long — our open declaring `max-frame-size` 4096 and `channel-max` 8, the peer's answering open, the begin pair landing `MAPPED`, both attaches, the peer's flow granting credit of ten, then a single 4097-octet frame on the session's own channel — passes steps one to seven in both artefacts and fails only at the eighth, where a *sent* frame one octet over the partner's declared maximum is **admitted by both layers**, while the same frame *received* is refused with `amqp:connection:framing-error`, class `limit`, the detail naming both numbers and the connection reaching `DISCARDING`. So the check exists in the shape it should and its mirror on the sending path is absent — one check, one path, with a working twin to copy from. The isolation is the achievement: seven steps arranged so that when the eighth fails, the size is the only thing that could have failed it. **And the diagnosis corrected the framing rather than the finding: the mechanism is present and the gap is a value or a measurement, not a missing check.** The comparison sits at `Spec/Connection.lean:1053` as `refuseUnless (size ≤ limits.maxFrameSize)`, worded directionally for a send; `limitsFor` routes an outbound frame to `endpoint.remoteLimits` unless the state's send class is conforming, and inbound ones to `localLimits`; and `remoteLimits` is written from the *received* open's own declared limits. All three links exist, and the mechanism demonstrably fires for the a-priori case — `exchange-pipelined-frame-size-limit` sends a frame one octet over `MIN-MAX-FRAME-SIZE` from an a-priori state and is refused with class `limit` in both artefacts. **My note had anticipated a missing comparison and that anticipation was wrong**; the two readings that remain are that `remoteLimits.maxFrameSize` is not 4096 by the time the frame is sent, or that the `size` the send path measures is not the octet count of the frame actually sent — the second of which would show as the *received* side passing while the sent side does not, which is the discriminator worth stating in the probe's own note. **And the diagnosis completed with the fix's shape and a shadowing instance sharper than any before it.** The comparison sits at `stepAmqpFrame` (line 1055) — but that function is reached only when the *connection* answers for a frame itself. A frame on a **session's channel** goes to the session: the codec's session branch runs the connection first for the frames it can carry, which relays and returns, then hands the body to the session's step **which is given no size at all — `Spec/Session.lean` contains no reference to a frame's size anywhere**. So the comparison is neither missing nor unreached: it sits on one of the two routes a frame can take. **And that corrects my own earlier claim**: the a-priori case that refuses in the corpus (`exchange-pipelined-frame-size-limit`) does *not* prove the mechanism fires, because a pre-negotiation frame is refused there by the *channel* rule rather than by the size. **A negative vector that passes for the wrong reason is the shadowing pattern in its purest form**, and it is the second time this stretch that the apparent proof of a mechanism turned out to be a neighbouring rule. The fix is where the check is *called* rather than what it *compares*: lift the comparison into the connection's submission dispatcher so it runs for every frame before any layer or role dispatch — which covers the session route, since the codec always gives the frame to the connection first — and `limitsFor` already routes to the partner's limits for a send, this peer's own for a receive, and the a-priori pair for a conforming state. **And the shadowing pattern now has its sharpest named instance.** `exchange-pipelined-frame-size-limit` expects a refusal one octet over `MIN-MAX-FRAME-SIZE` and gets one — from the **channel** rule, not from the size comparison, so it never proved that mechanism fires. That is the isolation rule's third instance and the strongest of them, because a vector that *expects* a refusal reads like evidence for the rule it names even when a different rule supplies the refusal, whereas the earlier instances were admissions that looked like the wrong rule firing. The test is unchanged and is what found it: **disable the rule the vector names, and see whether the vector fails.** The held eight-step vector is shaped so that it will, since its seven set-up steps isolate the size from every other rule — which is the same reason its author could say the answer was decisive in one run.

**And the queue's first two items are written down where the next sitting starts.** Lift the size comparison into the connection's submission dispatcher in both layers, with that vector as the pin; **then let the ledger follow the code**, since the dispositions naming `stepAmqpFrame` will name the dispatcher once the comparison moves — which is the discipline the gate has been enforcing all stretch, applied in the direction of a fix rather than a defect. Then the six proof branches, the assembly applying the nine, `flowLink` and `transferLink`, and the three session invariants, with the nine-goal list, the placement taxonomy and the escalation rule all recorded.**And my collision inference was wrong, in the family's own way.** I read the spelling "the carrier has landed and the gap is now the witness" in the entry's pre-edit state and concluded that the layer work had edited a planner-owned file. Its answer was factual rather than accommodating: `ledger/**` is off-limits in its own ticket in those words, it listed every file it touched, and it noted the phrase travelled in a message to the disposition agent — **so the route was permitted traffic carrying a reading, which is precisely what the boundary is for.** I had inferred *authorship* from a *wording*, a claim at one remove from its subject like the rest of this session's corrections; and the response worth noting is that it would rather be corrected on the facts than accept a fault it cannot see, which is the right instinct in both directions.**And the probe is another instrument worth keeping: flip the expectation so the harness prints the branch that answered.** Expecting `admitted` where the expectation is a refusal, or the reverse, makes the run print what actually happened and with which numbers, which distinguishes two hypotheses in one invocation. It is the same move that resolved the class-mismatch rendering earlier, used deliberately this time rather than discovered. **And a reported edit that did not land is the claims-side gap one level out.** The dispositions pass reported documenting in `conventions` that the register carries both spellings of a declaration path; a grep of every file's conventions block finds no such note anywhere. The claim was accurate in *intent* and absent from the *record* — the same shape as the false disposition, except that no gate can see it, because a claim made in a message has no field to check. The remedy is the one this session keeps arriving at: **a claim about a landed edit is verified by reading the file**, which is what happened here, and the note is now written into the file whose entries differ in spelling from the others. It is worth stating that no mechanism is proposed for this one: the ledger's gate checks claims *about the code*, the mutation controls check the *code*, and a claim in a message is checked by whoever holds the file — which is the arrangement, not a gap in it, as long as the reading actually happens.**Two record-keeping consequences, both of which point the discipline the other way.** The `max-frame-size.1` disposition names `Spec.Endpoint.maxFrameSizeSend…` as its carrier, and I had gone looking for a false claim there on the theory that it named a check which does not exist — **with the mechanism present, that theory is wrong, and the disposition stays**: a carrier that fails for one case is a bug in the carrier, not a missing carrier, and a disposition says what carries a clause. And the file's ownership question is resolved by the window having closed properly: `Spec/Connection.lean` is free, the sweep's vector waits behind the fix by design, and the layer work can take the probe's answer straight to a patch in both layers with the vector as the pin.**And the question of landing it red had the answer that the discipline is already satisfied.** The rule is that a new contract is run and *observed* failing before the implementation that satisfies it, and that observation now exists and is recorded — seven steps passing, the eighth failing at the sending side. What a red landing would add is a known-broken expectation inside the artifact a downstream implementation *reads*. So the fix goes first and the vector follows as a **witnessed pin**: the check biting rather than the hole open, which is the stronger artifact. Meanwhile the gap is recorded where the claims live, in the disposition for the clause, and the re-run's protocol is the one that keeps it honest — if step eight does not flip after the fix, that is a finding about the fix rather than a reason to edit the expectation to fit. **And the false claim turned out to be worse than a wrong name.** The disposition for `max-frame-size.1` named `Spec.Endpoint.maxFrameSizeSend` — a declaration that exists nowhere in either layer — **and its note read "send-side bound derived from the partner value exchanged in open"**, which is precisely the derivation the eight-step probe shows does *not* happen. So the entry did not merely name a missing carrier: **it asserted the behaviour that is broken**, which is the family's shape in its purest form yet — a claim sourced from the design intention rather than from the code. The correction names the declaration that holds the comparison (`Spec.Endpoint.stepAmqpFrame`, `Connection.lean:1028`, the comparison at 1053) in the convention the other entries use, and its note separates the two grades. **And the gate's acceptance criterion is complete in one line, assembled from four versions of the check getting it wrong in one direction or the other.** A `formalized:` value **resolves iff the declaration it names exists in the module the path names** — where declarations include **constructors** (not only `def`, `theorem`, `abbrev`, `structure` and `inductive`), and where the accepted spellings are the *derivable* abbreviations only: the root-namespace drop, the doubled join, and the file-plus-anchor form. Each part was learned by a version failing somewhere: two styles missed at first; the abbreviation absent, which would have reported correct entries as false; a rule that ignored the module path, which would have accepted `formalized:Spec.Nonsense.stepAmqpFrame`; and a keyword-only scanner flagging **`Spec.Connection.Submission.frame`, a constructor**, as unresolved. **Both directions are failures the gate must avoid** — a false positive against a correct entry is worse than no gate for a record people are meant to trust, and a false negative on `Spec.Endpoint.stepAmqpFrame`, which resolved-looking and named nothing, is the hole the gate exists to close. The first subject is measured: the slice file's 35 entries carry 40 `formalized:` instances and 24 `test:` instances over 18 distinct vectors, all resolving, which is the receipt the gate's run will be compared against — and any of those 40 reported as unresolved means the gate is wrong rather than the record.**Those two grades are worth a rule, because the earlier version of this plan had only one of them.** A carrier that *exists* and misses a case is **not** a false claim: the clause is carried, and the case is a bug in the carrier, which belongs in the plan and the fix. A carrier *named* that does not exist, or a note asserting a behaviour the carrier does not have, **is** false — a defect in the ledger, caught by reading the code and by no tier this repository has. That is the claims-side gap found live rather than named: the mutation controls mutate code, and the one claim checked by hand was wrong in both of its parts.**And a gap in this repository's own instruments that the sweep named without calling it one: the claims have no mutation control.** A disposition asserting that a clause is carried, when the check it names does not exist, is a false claim *inside the record* — and unlike a wrong specification line, nothing mechanical catches it, because V4 mutates *code*. The carrier mapping that found `idle-time-out.7` and `connections.5` was such a control performed by hand: a read-only pass asking, for every undispositioned clause, whether any declaration or vector carried it. That is worth stating plainly for the next slice: **every tier this repository has tests the code, and the claims are tested by reading.** **And the claims-side control now has a shape, supplied by the layer work applying the two-grade rule to its own modules.** Two forms, one small and one broad. The small one is mechanical: the ledger has **no existence gate for `formalized:` paths** — the disposition pass verified all 37 by hand, by grep, because nothing else does it — and a `formalized:` value naming a declaration that does not exist is exactly the false claim the corrected entry was. Every `formalized:` path should resolve to a declaration in the module it names, and every `test:` id to a vector, checked by the tool rather than by a careful reader. The broad one is a **sweep of docstrings that state mechanism and direction**, which is where the frame-size note lived and where three of the layer work's own are named for the next pass: `limitsFor`'s (verified against 871–876 when it was read for the size finding), the placement taxonomy added this stretch (verified against `place`, `placed` and the channel rule's site), and `Position.creditFor`'s conservation-law claim — the one of the three a *proof* rather than a reader will settle, since its lemma is still outstanding. **The argument for sweeping them is the one that made the disposition's error the strongest case of all: the claim was read only because it was quoted, and it had been wrong in both parts since it was written.****And the exit property went from one failing state to two, the second being the patch's side effect — found by the second instrument again.** `reach-end_rcvd` refused its mandated exit before and still does; `reach-end_sent` now refuses its own — the diagram's `R:END` into `UNMAPPED` — with "channel 1 is not this session's incoming channel, none". Its author is explicit that this is *not* the label-as-phase case the register rules on: the probe reaches `END_SENT` by the **ordinary route** — send begin, receive begin, send end — where a sent end releases the *outgoing* channel and leaves the *incoming* one, and both the state description and the diagram's arrow say the endpoint may receive there. So the incoming channel is gone on a route that should still have it, which is a finding about the patch and is routed to its author with that evidence. **Note what found it**: the same walk that produced the original finding, re-run on the settled tree because its earlier run was correctly withdrawn — a regression instrument noticing a fix's side effect rather than the fix. **And the mapping came back: 21 of the 35 clauses carry at least one carrier, with file and line, and 14 are uncarried** — ten of them Part 0's conformance framework, which are statements about what a conformant implementation is rather than rules this specification enforces, and four that are findings in the slice's own scope. **`connections.5`** ("prior to closing a connection, each peer MUST write a close frame with a code") has no carrier — no close frame is ever written — though the corpus has a pipeline-close vector, so that one is checked before it is called a defect. **`idle-time-out.7`** is not merely uncarried but **contradicted**: the artifact requires implementations to be prepared to handle empty frames on any valid channel, and `Spec.Frame.decodeFrame` always reads a described-type body and refuses anything else, so the specification refuses a frame the clause says must be handled. **`desired-capabilities.1`** has no capability handling in either layer. And **`sessions.1`** — a link MUST NOT be attached to more than one session — is *vacuously* satisfied by a one-link model, which is now registered as a reading rather than left as a gap: there is no second session for a link to be attached to, and a vector for a violation the model cannot express would be a test that cannot fail. **All three findings were checked by the layer's author, and two came back differently than the mapping said.** `idle-time-out.7` is confirmed as contradicted — and the confirmation came with the fact that makes the fix small: **a bodyless frame would be carried correctly by the session layer**, since a body that is no declared performative reaches the step function as an anonymous type with no mandatory fields and the arm that returns the session unchanged, so the refusal is the frame layer's alone and nothing has to change above `decodeFrame`. That file is the frame slice's rather than the session's, so it is routed there with the clause, the code path and the localisation. `connections.5` is the *narrower-cover* branch of the either/or: the corpus vector cites the clause and its six steps are all state transitions, so it carries the machine the clause sits beside rather than the clause's content — "each peer MUST write a close frame **with a code**" — and the implementation builds no close frame at all, the corpus supplying the frame the layer only transitions on. The disposition's note is corrected to say that, since "no close frame is written" would send a reader looking for the wrong thing. And `remote-outgoing-window` is confirmed as the **third** unread quantity: it appears in the structure, in the two rules that maintain it, and in prose quoting the doc, and in no guard, comparison or branch — so it joins `available` and `drain` in the same sentence of the windows contract's declared exclusions rather than in a new one. **And the bound audit closed with a withdrawal that corrects a claim of mine as well as its own.** Three vectors landed and verified (29 vectors, zero failures in both artefacts): the channel bound at declared plus one — the mutant-killer, witnessed; the window-versus-credit one *shaped* so the link's credit cannot answer first, which is what the shadowing required; and the handle bound at declared plus one, whose refusal names it exactly. The **MIN-MAX-FRAME-SIZE** entry is withdrawn on a reading: 512 is the maximum frame size *before explicit negotiation*, and the field's own text constrains the *sender* — "a peer MUST NOT send frames larger than its partner can handle. A peer that receives an oversized frame MUST close the connection" — so both layers admitting an open that declares 100 are correct, and the bound worth testing is the a priori one, which the oversized-frame probes already exercise. **My own note listed it as missing and is withdrawn with it.** **And the small half is now specified precisely enough to implement without re-deriving it.** Two rules in `check`, both mechanical and both failing loudly with the offending value named. **Resolves**: every `formalized:` value must name a declaration that exists under `lean/**`, collected by scanning the modules for `def`, `theorem`, `structure` and `inductive` names in the dotted forms the ledger uses — so a value like `Spec.Endpoint.maxFrameSizeSend` fails the gate instead of being found by hand six months later. **Carries**: every `test:` value must appear as a `vector` in some `vectors/*.ndjson`, so a citation to a vector that was renamed or never generated fails too. Both are the shape of the gates already in that script: **a claim that something exists, checked against the thing that would have to exist.** **The slice's dispositions are landed, and two more of my instructions were corrected by the record itself.** The 35 entries are committed — 21 carried by a declaration or a vector, 10 Part 0 framework clauses `informative`, 3 deferred with the gap named, `sessions.1` superseded — taking undispositioned MUST-class from 205 to 170 and the ledger's dispositions from 96 to 131. Both corrections were mine rather than the file's. My instruction named "the ten Part 0 conformance-framework clauses" and then **listed eleven refs**, of which `conformance.3` has a genuine carrier; the agent resolved it by the mapping rather than by my words, which is the right ordering, since dispositioning a carried clause as informative would have been a false claim in the direction nobody was looking. And my instruction to take `kind` from the coverage class produced seven entries reading `MUST` for clauses whose own keyword is `MUST NOT`. The rule is now in the file's own conventions block: **`kind` is the clause's keyword, `class` is what the coverage gate counts, and the two diverge exactly where a disposition would otherwise be read wrongly.** **And the existence gate's first run found 48 more claims of the same kind, with a documented reason and a triage now owed.** `part0`'s 37 values all resolve (two needing the doubled-namespace join the ledger uses for anchors), but four other disposition files carry **48 `formalized:` values naming declarations that exist nowhere under `lean/**`** — the planned modules from the plan's own layout (`Wire`, `Message`, `Endpoint`, the S1 value laws). They are not false claims by the record's own definition, because the conventions block of the earliest of those files says so in terms: *"At S0 the declaration does not exist yet: the disposition is a commitment made by the plan, and becomes checkable when `lean/Spec` lands (a declaration-existence gate is added at S1)."* **So the gate is the S1 promise being kept late, and the exemption belongs in the file's conventions rather than in a filename list in the tool** — with the exempted count printed rather than skipped, since silence about 48 claims is the same defect as silence about one. `Carries` gets no exemption: a `test:` id names something already committed. And the 48 then need triage the gate cannot do — each is either a planned module, named by a line in the plan's layout, or a mistyped name, named by the nearest declaration that exists — which is the difference between a commitment and a false claim and the reason the gate is worth having. **And the 48 triage into three classes rather than two, which changes what each one needs.** Checked in the tree: `lean/Spec/` holds `Codec`, `Connection`, `ConnectionCodec`, `Frame`, `FrameCodec`, `Main`, `ReadLaws`, `Session`, `SessionCodec` and `Value`, and **`Wire.lean`, `Message.lean` and `Endpoint.lean` do not exist** — though the plan's layout lists all three, so the implementation folded two of them elsewhere: the frame-header work is in `Frame.lean`, the state machines are in `Connection.lean` under an `Endpoint` namespace, and `Message.lean` is S5's module, scheduled and unwritten. So: **class 1** is genuine forward commitment — anything naming message sections or annotations, which the exemption and its printed count are for; **class 2** is a stale *path* rather than a promise — `Spec.Wire.*` or `Spec.Endpoint.*` where the declaration exists under a folded name, and **those want correcting, because a reader following them today finds nothing**, which is the same experience the false claim produced; **class 3** is a name resembling no declaration anywhere, which is false whatever the file's convention says and is the gate's real catch. And the scanner needs one more path style: the ledger abbreviates by **dropping the doubled structure component** (`SpecAMQP.Spec.Session.afterEnd` written `Spec.Session.afterEnd`, `SpecAMQP.Spec.Connection.Endpoint.stepAmqpFrame` written `Spec.Endpoint.stepAmqpFrame`), so without that rule the gate reports class 2 as class 3 and sends a reader hunting two dozen false claims that are stale paths.**And a repair lesson from the same stretch, in the other direction**: the empty-frame change's second failing site (`Spec/ConnectionCodec.lean`) needed no edit at all once the layer work read it — the mismatch was that the codec passed `frame.body` into a variant whose field was still `Value`, so widening that *field* fixed both codec sites and the *send* arm together, where patching the line the error named would have fixed one call site and left the other route's seam in place. **A repair that names the type beats a repair that names the line**, and both routes had the seam because a bodyless frame arrives by either.**That makes eleven instructions or claims of mine corrected in this session, every one by the party holding the subject and none of them expensive.** A field list inferred from a count; a fifth defect read out of a failing statement; a flow sibling doubted without opening the file; an over-general widening prediction; a hypothesis that a placement cleared a channel; a regression framing; a withdrawn bound; a missing-check anticipation; a receive-side carrier reading; an eleven-ref set described as ten; and a `kind` taken from the wrong column. The count is worth recording because the *cost* is the finding: each was a sentence, each was corrected within minutes by whoever held the code, and the expensive version of every one would have been believing it.**And the specification carries one fact that matters more than either rule**: the `formalized:` values in the ledger are written in *two* styles — a dotted declaration path like `Spec.Endpoint.stepAmqpFrame`, and a file-plus-anchor form pointing at `lean/Spec/Session.lean` — so the scanner must accept both, and **the gate must be run against the ledger's own values before it is trusted, because a gate that fires on correct entries is worse than no gate**. That is the family's shape in the other direction — a check whose *failure* carries no information — and it makes the acceptance criterion for the gate itself: `check` passes on the unchanged record, and fails when a `formalized:` value or a `test:` id is edited to something that does not exist. Both directions observed, not asserted.**The frame-layer clause is a genuine gap, and its fix carries a consequence sharper than the gap: a proved theorem that is true only because of the defect.** The reading is precise. For a header-only frame the decoder takes its body region as `bytes.extract start bytes.size` — the rest of the buffer rather than the frame's own SIZE window — so with the frame alone in the buffer that region is empty, the value read refuses at zero fuel, and `decodeFrame` **translates the truncated read into `sizeMismatch`** ("the performative at octet 8 does not complete within the 8 octets SIZE declares"). `malformed` is the *other* case — a body whose first octet is not a described value — so the class I relayed was wrong, and the disposition is being corrected with it, since the refusal class is what the differential compares. The clause does bind: "it MAY send an empty frame … Implementations MUST be prepared to handle empty frames arriving on any valid channel … Apart from this use, empty frames have no meaning." An empty frame is legal traffic a receiver must not fail on, and the second sentence says it carries no performative — so the right decode result is a frame the session can answer without acting. **And my absence report was wrong, which produced the family's newest shape: a claim true when written and false when read, with no edit in between.** The path-spelling note *was* landed — in `conventions["formalized:"]` rather than in a key of its own, and carried into the repository by my own commit because the change was a working-tree edit made after the previous one.
