My grep missed it twice over: I searched for "spelling" and "form" while the note says "fully qualified" and "module-relative", and I read the file rather than its history. **`git log -- <path>` then `git show <rev>:<path>` settles that in one step**, which is the habit the dispositions pass named after I named the other collisions: **check the history before filing an absence as a defect, exactly as reading the file is worth doing before believing a landed edit.** Both directions caught something real within the hour — the stale path in `max-frame-size.1`, and this note that looked absent and was not — and the difference between the two is that one was a defect and the other was a search that could not have found what was there. **And the tree's uncommitted set is now one slice per owner, with nothing unaccounted for.** The 31-octet probe is tracked, which makes the plan's citation of it true of a checkout rather than only of a working tree; the generator went in with the corpus it generates, since a byte-identity check can only be re-run by someone who has the source; and the layer work committed its own five files — the `BEGIN_SENT` label branch, the placement widening at both sites with the channel-map fix, the bodyless-frame seams, and the credit invariant's state with its lemmas — while **deliberately leaving the rest and naming the owners**: the frame work in `Spec/Frame*`, `Ref/Frame` and the codec twins with `Proofs/CodecFrameLaws.lean` and the new assembly module, and the ledger's dispositions with the existence gate under `scripts/`. That is the discipline the repository asks for in one movement — **a path belongs to exactly one owner, and a slice commits its own files when it is accepted** — and it is the same statement the existence gate makes about claims, one directory over.     **And the git discipline gains its fourth form, named precisely by the interlocking.** The repository's notes already say *stage and commit in one command, never leave a staged set sitting*, and this stretch has shown four ways of breaking it: **a script that printed success without checking** (two of mine, whose anchors were absent so nothing landed); **a pipeline whose exit status came from its last command** (`… gate.sh | grep | tail && git commit` committed whatever the gate said); **a commit whose summary was trusted over its content** (a stat line reporting four files and 1223 insertions while carrying fewer changes); and **an `amend` without a pathspec, which in a shared tree absorbs whatever a peer has staged.** That last is the sharpest, because the index is shared and an amend reads all of it: the four files were not lost, they were *taken* — and the party who took them did not know whose they were while the party who lost them still saw them as unstaged, which is why one `git status` between us found what neither report contained. **The safe forms are a pathspec on every amend, or a commit immediately after each add**, and the tell when it happens anyway is the one both halves of this accident displayed: **two counts that should differ, agreeing.****And a matching count was the tell that both of us read past.** A commit's summary reported four files and 1223 insertions while its content carried fewer changes, and the sign was that the *unstaged* diff afterwards showed **the same 1223** — two counts that should have differed agreeing, which reads as a benign artifact and was the finding. What settled it was `git show <rev>:<path>` with a grep against the working tree, which is the differential this session keeps reaching for: **a commit's stat line is a claim about a commit, and the only source for it is the commit's content.** `git log`, `--stat` and any count taken from either are summaries, and summarising standing in for the thing is the whole family of this session's corrections. **And the two events interlock**: the amend that took a staged set took *exactly those four files*, staged and uncommitted, so each of us saw one half of a single accident without knowing the other half existed — and one `git status`, run by the party who had not caused it, is what surfaced both.

**And the ledger's claims are all accounted for, which is the first time that has been true.** The gate is landed with its counts printed, the slice's 35 dispositions are in, the `unkeyed` file now declares its `formalized:` vocabulary so its single such value is *classified* rather than skipped, and the carriers that named a namespace-that-is-not-one are corrected to the declarations that hold them. The census reads **40 values checked, 55 commitments exempt and counted, 24 vector ids checked — and the value that had been invisible sits inside the exempt count rather than outside the census**, which is the difference between a claim the record has classified and one it has not looked at. Two of this session's corrections met in that number: the false disposition that started the gate, and the exemption question whose answer was that a commitment is a property of the claim rather than of the file it is written in.

**And the existence gate is landed and green, with the qualities worth naming explicitly.** It collects the derivable spellings and *nothing looser* — "tails, bare names and invented module paths do not resolve" — collects constructors alongside `def`, `theorem`, `lemma`, `structure`, `inductive`, `abbrev`, `class`, `instance` and `opaque`, exempts a claim that any commitment-declaring file declares a commitment, and **scopes the existence rules to the repository's own ledger**, because a fixture ledger describes a planted artifact with no tree beside it; that scoping is what keeps the ledger contract's own fixture case green. It prints its counts rather than only its verdict, and its two failure witnesses are in the plan's own currency: `formalized:Spec.Endpoint.maxFrameSizeSend` — the example this plan wrote — and a `test:` id renamed to a vector that does not exist, both caught, both reverted by exact substitution with the restoration asserted byte-identical, and **no `git checkout` used on any disposition**.

**And three findings from the existence gate's first green, each a different way a check can be quiet.** *Marker classification*: the gate decides whether a file's `formalized:` values are *present* or *commitments* by phrases in its conventions text, so a file whose convention says "resolved against" rather than "verified present" is classified as documenting **no vocabulary** — its 40 values are **not resolved at all**, and the outcome reads as a green. That is the quietest of the standings the gate has and the one a reader is least likely to notice, and it is the argument for printing the *count* checked rather than only the verdict. *Constructors are the one declaration form a `formalized:` value cannot name*, and they are exactly where a widened field lives — so the natural name for a type-shape change is the one the gate rejects; the rule is to name the type and describe the constructor in the note, since a scanner accepting any `| name` line would resolve `Spec.Nonsense.frame` and be too permissive in the other direction. *And a file with no `formalized:` convention at all* (`unkeyed-normative.json`) carries one such value, reported as undeclared rather than checked, which one line turns into verified. **And the frame slice closed as one movement across two owners, with the minutes between them disclosed rather than hidden.** FrameProofs committed its nine paths and I committed the contract; the library builds at 48 jobs and the slice reads as one change in the log while being two commits by necessity. **For the minutes between them the repository's committed state did not build** — the new proof against the unrestated contract — and that interval is in the commit message, because it is what a two-owner slice looks like when its halves land separately and neither author can complete it alone. The commit that followed records the same thing in the opposite direction: my half was the one that had been missing, and the tree was red until it landed.

**And the integration caught two of the eleven inventory names I added, for a reason worth keeping.** The gate rejected `SpecAMQP.Proofs.readScalarData_progress` and `…exists_of_bind_ok` as unknown constants, and both exist — under `SpecAMQP.Proofs.ReadProgress`, the module's own namespace, which the list they came from had flattened. So the gate's first catch was a transcription error in the *inventory* rather than a missing theorem, which is the class of the false disposition and the stale path once more: **a name copied along with its list's shorthand.** The paths were corrected by substitution rather than by re-adding, so the line count is unchanged and a reviewer sees exactly two words move.

**And the bad commit landed because a pipeline's exit status came from `tail`.** `… gate.sh | grep -v warning | tail -2 && git commit` commits whatever the gate said, because `&&` reads the last command's status — the same defect the plan recorded an hour earlier in two of my own scripts, appearing this time in a shell invocation, which is a sign the shape is about *reporting* rather than about any one tool. The correction is the same in both places: **read the exit status of the thing being checked, not of whatever prints its output.****And two of my own scripts printed success without checking, which is the same defect one level down.** An "inventory wired" run and a later attempt at the same edit both announced completion while their anchors were absent, so the replacement was a no-op and nothing landed — the second caught only because a subsequent grep found none of the expected names in the file it had supposedly edited. **A script that reports success unconditionally is a check that cannot fail**, and the remedy is the one this session keeps arriving at: assert the anchor, print the count, and verify against the artefact rather than against the script's own message.

**And the git discipline was mine to relearn rather than to teach.** An `amend` of mine took a staged set — the trap this repository's own notes describe in terms — and left one commit's content and message out of step; the message was corrected rather than the record, since an admitting message beats a log nobody can audit. Two slices' files were briefly staged together, which is why the frame slice's commit is being coordinated as one movement across two owners rather than as two attempts at it.**The tree's state is therefore one red gate with a known cause rather than an unknown one**: `Proofs/CodecFrameLaws.lean` does not compile until its three `.body` uses land, which is why `s1_proof_integrity` fails at its build step while both of its scans pass and the corpora are green in both artefacts. A red whose owner and cause are both known is a different object from a red nobody can explain, and the difference is the reason every gate in this repository prints what it rejected.**And the duplicated rule was reconciled rather than left standing twice**: the spelling rule lives once, in the `path-spelling` key, and `formalized:` keeps only what is not a duplicate — "every path recorded here was resolved against the module it names, and every `test:` id against the corpus, rather than inferred from the carrier's name" — which is a receipt about what was done to the file rather than a restatement of a convention, and a better sentence than either of us had written for it.**Two consequences its owner found, both of which shape the change.** Because the body region is the rest of the buffer, **an empty frame ahead of another frame makes a reader look at the next frame's octets** — so the empty test must be on the frame's own window, SIZE equal to the header length with DOFF at its minimum, rather than on a truncated read. That is a requirement rather than a suggestion: it is the difference between a carrier that works on one frame and one that works on a stream. And the send/receive asymmetry is separable — sending an empty frame is a MAY while handling one is a MUST — so the writer's domain, and with it the round-trip law, is untouched and the change stays on the acceptance path.

**And the consequence worth the whole report**: `AcceptedFramesCarryPerformatives` is stated and proved, and it says that every accepted frame's body is a described value carrying a performative — **which is true today only because a header-only frame is refused.** Accepting one falsifies it. **A proved theorem whose truth depends on a defect is a green light wired to the bug**, and it is the sharpest instance of this session's family: the check is not merely unable to fail, it is true *because of* what it cannot see. The remedy is a restatement as a **disjunction** rather than an exclusion — an accepted frame is either bodyless or a described value naming a performative — which keeps the claim about *every* accepted frame that the contract's own prose says is the point, and which is the artifact's own structure given that an empty frame "apart from this use has no meaning". It lands as **one patch**: the decode path and the bodyless representation, the proof's restatement, the contract and its prose, the acceptance module and the inventory line — since the change alone falsifies the statement and a restatement alone would be false today.**One bound is unresolved and shadowed, and the ruling keeps it out of the corpus until it is not.** A 4097-octet frame against a declared maximum of 4096 is refused in both layers — but by the *session's channel* rule, class `illegalState`, rather than by the size rule, which would carry class `limit`. So either the size check is genuinely shadowed by the channel check, or the probe never presents an oversized frame to it, and its author will not assert which. **The probe is the thing to fix first**: present the oversized frame on the session's own channel, so no channel rule can answer, and then the result is decisive — the check exists and the vector is a pin, or it is missing and the vector is a failure-first contract with a fix behind it. It is not landed meanwhile, because a corpus that is red for a reason nobody can act on is worse than one that is silent about the case, which is the same call that removed two broken vectors earlier in this stretch. `arrayElementLimit` stays unresolved rather than missing, on the ground its author gave: the check for it is crude and 65537 elements are impractical to express.**And the register entry for `sessions.1` gained the reason it is vacuous**: the model has no session-side multiplicity at all — a session *is* one link's worth of state — and the clause becomes expressible when a session holds a link registry, which is a later slice's shape. Registering it now and expressing it there is the same call as `available` and `drain`: **record the exclusion where the model is silent, and revisit at the slice that gives it a subject.** The Part 0 clauses as `informative` were agreed on the same grounds — they describe what a conformant implementation is rather than constraining this specification's machines, and **none of them has a carrier by construction rather than by omission**.**One finding matters to the windows contract directly**: `session-flow-control.4` and `.8` compute `remote-outgoing-window`, **a quantity nothing in either layer reads**, so no vector can witness them — the same position `peerCount` and `peerCredit` were in, and the same conclusion follows: the invariant is the only instrument that can hold it. The windows contract therefore gains a second declared exclusion beside `available` and `drain`, for the same reason and with the same wording: a quantity the layer maintains and no rule consults is carried by its law or by nothing.

**The carriers give the dispositions their content**: `Spec/Connection.lean`'s state and step functions for the connection clauses, `Spec/Session.lean`'s `Windows.afterSendingTransfer`/`afterReceivingTransfer`/`afterReceivingFlow` and the placement sites for the session ones, `Contracts/TypeSystem.lean`'s constructor grammar for the single Part 0 clause that has a genuine carrier, and seventeen named vectors for the behavioural clauses. The dispositions are being written from that mapping rather than from memory, and the four uncarried findings are routed rather than dispositioned away.**And the bound audit returned the missing list: five of seven bounds have no vector at exactly one step outside them.** Nothing sits at `channel-max` plus one (the channel mutant's home), at `max-frame-size` plus one octet, at an open declaring below `MIN-MAX-FRAME-SIZE`, or at `handle-max` plus one; the a priori channel bound and the length ceilings have vectors. **The `handle-max` entry refines a claim of mine**: the corpus's `exchange-session-handle-range-*` vectors declare a bound of 2 and attach handle 5, which kills a *runaway* bound but not one widened by one, since 5 is refused by the true bound and the widened bound alike. **Only bound + 1 distinguishes them, and that is the whole content of the rule** — a vector *inside* the bound is not a test of the bound, which is the family's shape applied to boundary claims and to my own note about them. `arrayElementLimit` comes back **unresolved rather than absent**, on the honest ground that the check for it is crude and 65537 elements is impractical to express: a three-valued answer where two would have been tidier.**And the machine now has a refusal taxonomy as a fact rather than an impression**: a **wire-level** refusal — the channel map — takes no placement and leaves the state as it is; a **session-level** refusal goes through `placed`; a **link-level** one through `place`. Three sites, three behaviours, and the corpus reached the third only once a vector was written that a *map* could not answer. The corpus stands at 71 vectors and 278 step verdicts, and the file is free for the sweep's two vectors and the witness window it asked for.**And the corpus-versus-instrument question was answered as a choice rather than defaulted, in the right direction.** A sweep probe is instrumentation — the correct home for the check its author needs while working. The corpus is the *delivered artifact*: a downstream implementation reads the corpus and never the sweep, so **a rule with no corpus vector is a rule a conforming implementation can get wrong without anyone's test noticing**, which is precisely what the channel mutant did in surviving every tier. The two suite-defect vectors therefore belong in the corpus rather than in the probe, hand-authored alongside the slice's exchange vectors, with the probe keeping its own copy for the working check — and the decision being recorded as a decision, since the alternative reading was defensible and would have left a real gap looking deliberate. **And the taxonomy is now recorded where the predicate lives, in both layers**, which is the right home for it: a wire-level refusal is raised outside the placement path and leaves the state where it is — the channel map, the decoder's own refusals; a session-level refusal goes through `placed`; a link-level one, raised by `flowLink`, `transferLink` and `detachLink`, through `place`. The note attached to it is the practical half: **a rule added at the link level wants a vector keyed on that rule rather than on the channel it arrives by**, since the corpus reached that third site only once such a vector existed. The rule its author took from the episode is the one the plan's own over-general sentence produced — a prediction that contradicts a reading should send its author to the reading — and the exit regression is with both instruments: routed to the layer with its evidence, and being re-read by its author against the placement distinction before it is called a finding.

**One correction to that re-read, since it matters which axis the walk is on**: the walk's complaint is about the **incoming map**, not the label — the refusal text is "channel 1 is not this session's incoming channel, none" on a route where it should still be one. So the placement taxonomy explains *landing states* and does not explain a *cleared map*; the question to answer is which step cleared `incoming` on the ordinary route, and the first hypothesis is that a placement firing where it should not takes the entry with it, since `DISCARDING`'s description says its incoming frames are discarded. **And the exit regression is not a regression: the walk's probe is on a route the ruling licenses, and both of my own hypotheses in this episode were disproved by the holder of the subject within the hour.** The ordinary route works, and the evidence is a *passing* vector rather than an argument: `exchange-session-begin-then-end` is exactly send begin, receive begin landing `MAPPED` with incoming channel 1, send end landing `END_SENT`, and its fourth step receives an end expecting `UNMAPPED`, admitted — green in both artefacts. What refuses an exit is a probe on the **`BEGIN_SENT` route**, where the state's own description says there is *no entry in the incoming channel map*, so the sent end that moves the label to `END_SENT` leaves the map empty and a received frame cannot arrive on a channel that was never mapped. That is the same description pair the register rules on, so under that ruling the refusal is correct and the walk's expectation is the model adjustment — symmetric with the six cells, not a side effect of the patch. **Two retractions in one hour, one from each side, and the second produced an instrument fix worth more than the finding would have been.** The exit-property failure was the walk's *route*, not the patch: `reach-end_sent`'s steps are send begin, **send end**, then the mandated received end — the `BEGIN_SENT` route — where the description says there is no incoming entry, so the sent end leaves the map empty and a received frame cannot arrive on a channel never mapped. The refusal is correct and the expectation was the thing to change, symmetric with the six cells. `reach-end_rcvd` had the mirror cause: a breadth-first path arriving through `BEGIN_RCVD`, whose outgoing channel is likewise never assigned, rather than through `MAPPED` where the corpus's vector passes. **And the fix went into the instrument**: the walk now tries *every* admitted entry rather than the shortest one, so a state is judged on its routes rather than on the path BFS happened to find. **A breadth-first walk's shortest path is not the route a description names** — the shape that produced a published finding an hour before it was retracted, by an author who then said the trace should have come before the report. That is right, and sharper than it looks: the verdict was never the question, the path was.

**So the exit property stands: every mandated exit holds on the route its description names**, with the two short entries refusing for the reasons the descriptions give. A slice acceptance item is therefore discharged — and it was discharged by discovering that its failure had been an artifact of how the walk searched, which is the same lesson as the six cells and the boundary value that was not a subject.

**And the boundary witness is captured, the window closed.** With mutant 5 planted and the build clean at 51 jobs, the sweep reported `driven-channel-above-the-declared-bound#3` — `ref refused / limit / OPENED / amqp:connection:framing-error` against `spec admitted / UNMAPPED` — the vector added for exactly this case, killing a mutant that had survived every tier. Reverted by text: no markers anywhere, both executables building at 51 jobs, the differential passing, the connection sweep back to zero divergences. **The record reads *killed by the new vector* rather than *survived***, which is the difference a mutation control exists to produce, and it is the first boundary in this specification shown closed rather than merely located.**And my second hypothesis went the way of the first, settled by the code rather than by a run**: the placement path sets `state` and nothing else — neither `place` nor `placed` writes a channel — so a placement cannot take the incoming entry with it whatever predicate it keys on. **The only branches in either layer that touch `incoming` are the begins that set it, the received end that clears it while moving to `END_RCVD`, and the resets to `UNMAPPED`** — four kinds of site, which is a bound on the search rather than a description of it. Both of my claims were offered as hypotheses and both were killed cheaply, which is what hypotheses are for; the expensive version would have been believing either of them and writing it into a vector.**Its bound-boundary audit's first pass proved nothing, and it said so rather than reporting coverage.** Searching the corpora for the boundary *values* found a match for every bound — but the matches are incidental: a single `slice.ndjson` line carries several of those numbers, and a string of 256 octets appearing somewhere is not a vector *about* the `str8` ceiling. **A value appearing is not a subject being tested**, which is the family's shape again, in the audit of the audit. The pass that means something names, per bound, the vector whose *subject* is that bound and whose value sits exactly one step outside it — `channel-max` at declared plus one, `max-frame-size` at its minimum minus one, the element limit at 65537, each length ceiling at 256 — and reports the ones that do **not** exist rather than the ones that matched.**And the patch's four parts are applied with two predictions standing.** The `BEGIN_SENT` branch lands with the `Ref` twin's text and comment. The `placed` widening is now keyed on the state's own receive permission — `State.mayReceive`, the reference's `receiveAllowed` — with `sessions.5` and `sessions.6` cited at both sites, which is better than the `mapped` test it replaces in the way that matters: the predicate now says what the clause says. The expectation change moves one step, and the isolating vector is `exchange-session-refusal-in-end-sent-discards`, whose first step is a flow naming handle 7 refused by the *handle* rule in `END_SENT` — a rule rather than the channel map, so the widened predicate is tested rather than shadowed — with the peer's end as its second step, the one frame a discarding session answers. The predictions to check: the corpus green in both artefacts with the new verdict count, and **no step moving except that one expectation**, since every other received refusal in the corpus arrives while the session is `MAPPED`. Anything else moving is a state nobody has thought about and is to be brought rather than folded in.**A channel bound widened by one survived everything, because a bound widened by one shows only at exactly bound + 1** — and no vector in the corpus, and no probe in the sweep, names a channel the bound refuses. The vector is exact: an `open` declaring `channel-max` 1, then a frame on channel 2. That is a rule about boundary claims rather than about channels, and it applies to every bound in the specification.

**And removing the window spend survived because the link's credit answers those vectors first** — the shadowing met four times now — but its survival inside the *sweep* had a cause its author owned: those cells had been converted to *observations* after the credit defeated the probe construction, and **an instrument that records cannot fail**. The fix is to arrange the credit correctly and predict the cells again, which is a change to the probe rather than to the model. Mutant 1's survivor is settled the same way: the differential missed it, the sweep killed it with a twenty-one-cell signature, and with the tree fixed the family is 21/21 refused again, leaving only the four `BEGIN_SENT` cells whose cause is the missing spec line.

**The mutation window is closed and the files returned.** No mutant planted, no marker anywhere, the snapshots deleted, `lean/Spec/**` and the generated tables back with the layer work — whose four-part patch (the `BEGIN_SENT` branch, the `placed` widening, the one expectation change, the isolating vector) now has an owner, a free file and a green tree. **And the corpus is single-writer while that patch lands**: the sweep's two suite-defect vectors wait for it, so that the differential never measures a corpus two people are editing.**And the window invariants now have their raw material, read from the section rather than recalled.** `session-flow-control` states eight things, dividing cleanly into the *claims* a contract can make and the *scope* it must declare. The claims: the **flow-update law** — `next-incoming-id` and `remote-outgoing-window` are set *directly* from the frame's `next-outgoing-id` and `outgoing-window`, a MUST stated twice in the section; the **send accounting** — a transfer increments `next-outgoing-id`, decrements `remote-incoming-window`, and MAY decrement `outgoing-window` depending on policy; the **receive accounting** — a received transfer advances `next-incoming-id` to the transfer's implicit id plus one and decrements `remote-outgoing-window`, with the same policy-May on `incoming-window`; and the two **recomputation** MUSTs — each window "decremented after every transfer sent/received, and recomputed when informed of the remote session endpoint state". The scope: `next-outgoing-id` is initialised arbitrarily and incremented under **RFC-1982 serial arithmetic**, which the model does not implement, since the specification's windows and ids are `Nat` and the wrap is therefore outside anything a theorem here can claim; and the two policy-May-decrements are held as `startPolicy`, a visible choice the corpus runs with, the other branch being conforming too.

**What a contract should claim, and what it should not.** Not a conservation identity: the windows decrement under guards — the sweep measures one as `amqp:session:window-violation` with class `limit` — and the state does not hold the last flow's values between flows, so an identity would need them. What is theorem-shaped is the **update laws** the artifact states as MUSTs, and the **no-wrap property** — a window reaches zero and refuses rather than wrapping, which is the credit law's gate in a different arithmetic. Both were confirmed as the shape to write by the same reasoning that produced the strengthened credit statement, and this time before a proof attempt rather than after one.## The supervisor's direction, and what it changes

**All five decisions were answered, with reasons, and the governing principle is one sentence: do not spend specification effort on implementation scaffolding while major parts of the AMQP semantic surface remain unwritten.** Recorded with the consequences taken rather than noted:

* **D1 — S5 messaging begins beside the S4 proof work**, not after it, and the socket runner is not built first. The guardrail is explicit: *do not let S5 become an excuse to leave S4's invariants permanently unfinished*, so the two are tracked as separate streams with the S4 obligations visible until closed. The sequencing it asks for is sections → transfer payload → delivery state → settlement, which is also the smallest message-bearing vertical slice.
* **D2 — V3 stays strictly third-party**: an implementation independently authored and *not generated from this model*, captured as an immutable vector and compared against the specification's prediction. A separately authored model in another prover may be useful but is classified separately as **V3b — independent formal cross-model**, never silently merged into V3. The reason is the finding this repository already owns: two internally independent artefacts can share one omission, so V3 must test what nothing local can.
* **D3 — the UTF-8 lemma stays an explicit conditional premise.** Not a subproject, and not a reason to reimplement a UTF-8 proof stack merely to remove one hypothesis. Upstreaming is library improvement if cheap, not programme work.
* **D4 — the one-link session stays for now**, with the safeguard that the restriction must be *explicit* rather than implied: recorded as `MODEL RESTRICTION: the S4 slice contains at most one link per session`, with the clauses it touches classified as fully modelled, vacuous under the restriction, or deferred until a registry exists — and widening triggered by a concrete need (multiple simultaneous deliveries, handle uniqueness becoming semantically active, per-link settlement, or a normative S5 clause the model cannot represent) rather than by anticipation.
* **D5 — no server as a deliverable.** A socket runner after S5 is a *consumer and demonstration*, explicitly not fidelity evidence and not V3. The architecture keeps the boundary `transport → octet stream → executable semantics` rather than embedding socket semantics in the specification, which is what gives downstream consumers their freedom of runtime and language.

**The model restriction and the unread quantities are classified rather than described.** `MODEL RESTRICTION: the S4 slice models at most one link per session.` Its consequences are already recorded as readings — a second `attach` on a live link naming another handle is admitted, and a link with both endpoints as sender is admitted — and the clauses it touches classify as: **fully modelled** (the connection, session and frame rules the corpus exercises), **vacuous under the restriction** (`sessions.1`, link uniqueness, registered as `link-uniqueness-vacuous`), and **deferred** (anything requiring a link registry, which the supervisor's D4 names four triggers for). Nothing else in the slice depends on the restriction.## Ownership, delegation, and the parallel wave

**The repository's ownership rule says `lean/Spec/**` and `lean/Contracts/**` are planner-owned, that coders and reviewers must not modify them, and that a changed file "requires an explicit planner update plus a regenerated SHA-1 manifest". Two things follow, and both are being made true rather than noted.**

* **The update, in both directions.** This plan is that explicit update for the session's edits to `lean/Spec/**` — the `BEGIN_SENT` label branch, the placement widening at both sites, the frame layer's empty-frame accept path, the bodyless seams in the dispatchers and the widened `Submission.frame` body, the `atState` channel maps, the role-direction and condition corrections, and the `handles` docstring — **ratified here, with their diffs in the log**, since the work was directed by this plan slice by slice and reviewed in each case. Every slice below carries an **explicit delegation** naming the files a coder may write, which is what the rule asks for and what the session had been leaving implicit.
* **The mechanism the rule names and which does not exist.** `toolchain/spec-manifest.sha1` hashes every planner-owned file — `lean/Spec/**`, `lean/Contracts/**`, `vectors/**`, `ledger/**` — and `tests/contracts/s0_spec_manifest.sh` recomputes it and compares, so a change to a planner-owned file is *visible* rather than merely against the rules. Regeneration is deliberately manual: the manifest changing is a planner act with a diff in the log, which is the point of having one.

**S5-message, the rungs in the supervisor's order.** `lean/Spec/Message.lean` and `lean/Ref/Message.lean` hold the Part 3 section types — `header`, `delivery-annotations`, `message-annotations`, `properties`, `application-properties`, `data`, `amqp-sequence`, `amqp-value`, `footer` — and the outcome state machine (`received`, `accepted`, `rejected`, `released`, `modified`, with settlement). Rungs: **1** the section types as data, table-driven where the artifact declares them; **2** the section codec in both artefacts; **3** the outcome types and their state machine; **4** the corpus, hand-authored exchanges beside the generated ones; **5** the differential over them. It depends on nothing in S3 — sections are values carried in a transfer's payload — which is why it runs beside the connection work rather than after it.

**S6-transactions as its own family.** `lean/Spec/Transactions.lean` and its `Ref` twin: the declare/discharge performatives, their state machine, and the session dispatch's new arm. It needs only the session dispatch, which nothing else in this wave holds.

**The generator split is what unblocks parallel corpus work**, and it is a prerequisite rather than housekeeping: `scripts/gen/values.py`, `frames.py` and `slices.py` with the two corpus generators as CLI-identical dispatching entry points — `scripts/gen-value-vectors.py` (values and frames) and `scripts/gen-exchange-vectors.py` (the slice families) — while `scripts/gen-oasis-lean.py`, the declared-surface table generator the tables gate names, stays as it is. The contracts invoke the corpus generators by path, so the shims are part of the split rather than a convenience on top of it, because *until it lands, families are added one slice at a time and the second waits*.

**And the wave's own first hour produced a dispatch rule, which is recorded because it cost three withdrawn attributions.** A dispatch to an agent states only what is **quoted** from the plan or from that agent's own report — never a paraphrase of a remembered report, because a paraphrase arrives with the authority of an instruction and the content of a guess. Within an hour of the wave's dispatch, three attributions had to be withdrawn: an `I64`/`Usize` obligation and an `h_len` follow that appear nowhere in the tree, and a set of proof-rung names belonging to another slice, plus two vector names composed for a third agent. Each was caught by the receiving agent grepping its own files, which is the check that should have preceded the message.**A label is derived from its evidence cell, never from a word appearing in it; a count is reconciled against the thing counted rather than against the table's own rows; and a schema's field is filled with the schema's word for that value rather than a sentence about it.** The isolation table produced the first of these three times in three hands — a row labelled `broad` while its experiment cell described the witness, twice, repaired by hand each time, and a first automated pass that read the phrase "no disabling experiment run" as evidence that a run happened, labelling 54 vectors witnessed where the evidence covers nine and caught only by reconciling the totals against the corpus. The register produced the second: an `adopted` field holding a paraphrase where the schema requires one of the `readings` names verbatim, which `clause-ledger.py check` rejected outright. Both are the dispatch rule above in another costume — **the artifact that records the evidence is not the evidence** — and in each case the check that caught it compared a claim against its source rather than against another claim.**And S5 and S6 each carry the supervisor's mutation requirement**: one structural, one semantic-boundary, one omission and one acceptance/rejection-polarity mutant, planted with the slice rather than after it, because the question is what *class* of wrong specification the evidence would fail to notice.**And the five quantities no rule consults are placed in the supervisor's four categories, which is the classification that stops dead state accumulating unnoticed.** `peerCount` and `peerCredit`: **maintained and invariant-checked** — the credit law's equation is stated through the same constructor that writes them, and it is what caught a defect in the branch that set one without the other. `remote-outgoing-window`: **maintained, and to be invariant-checked** — the windows contract's update laws and no-wrap property are the carriers, which is why the contract's statement is written before its proof. `available` and `drain`: **maintained but currently semantically inert** — consulted only by the field-level coupling rule that forbids setting either without a handle, and declared out of scope for the protocol rules this slice implements, so no vector can witness them and none claims to. **None is a candidate for removal yet**, and each is recorded where a reader meets it rather than only here.**A generated artifact is corrected in its generator, and the citation repair above was not.** `vectors/generated-exchanges.ndjson` is output; the twelve link-family vectors' clause lists live in `scripts/gen/slices.py`, and repairing the committed file alone means the next regeneration silently reverts the fix — after which the citation gate fails for whoever regenerated rather than for whoever made the edit. The generator slice measured this precisely (the pre-split generator and the split produce identical bytes and both differ from HEAD by the same 42 lines), ruled the split innocent, and holds the mapping; its generator-side half is the fix. And the class now has a gate: `tests/contracts/s0_generator_fidelity.sh` regenerates all three corpora into a scratch directory and compares bytes, which is what should have caught this rather than a peer's diligence.**And seven requirements follow, all of which this plan now carries as requirements:****And a generated corpus is compared against its generator, not against a previous copy of itself.** When the generator-side half of the citation repair landed, byte-identity with the hand-edited file turned out to be unattainable and *undesirable*: the hand-edit had mixed two escapings, and the artifact's convention — uniform `\u` escaping — is what every other committed `.ndjson` under `vectors/` follows. The gate was right to be red and the fix was a regeneration rather than a restoration, which is the whole point of comparing against the generator: it is the target, and a copy of the output is not.1. **The model restriction is declared** where the model is silent, with the clause classification above, so a reader cannot take one link per session for a property of AMQP.
2. **The ledger gains an `invariant` disposition** beside `formalized:` and `test:`, because some clauses are carried by a law about state rather than by a declaration or a vector — precisely the unread quantities. The supervisor's instruction is that coverage must state *how* a clause is witnessed, and that a generic "covered" category would hide the difference; this ledger has never had one, and now has the category its two existing ones could not express.
3. **The isolation rule becomes a permanent test-design requirement**: a negative vector counts as evidence for rule R only if disabling R makes the vector stop failing *for R's reason*. Where isolating a vector is impractical, it is classified as **broad conformance evidence** rather than rule-isolated evidence — a label rather than a claim.
4. **Every new semantic layer requires four mutation classes**, not a mutation count: one structural, one semantic-boundary, one omission, and one acceptance/rejection-polarity mutant. The point is to keep asking what *class* of wrong specification the current evidence would fail to notice.
5. **Unread quantities are classified four ways** — maintained and invariant-checked, maintained but currently semantically inert, deferred semantic use, or a candidate for removal — so dead semantic state cannot accumulate unnoticed.
6. **The TemperMint boundary is explicit and versioned**: SpecAMQP owns protocol semantics, clause interpretation, executable reference semantics, specification proofs, corpora, mutation controls and the ambiguity ledger; TemperMint owns the Rust implementation, translation, refinement proofs, certificates, native build binding and performance; and the handoff is a named interface rather than a convention.
7. **The work is tracked as five tracks** — S4 proofs, S5 semantics, V4 mutations per new slice, V3 evidence opportunistically, the UTF-8 lemma kept conditional — rather than as one queue, so that finishing S4 cannot disappear behind starting S5 and V3 can be pursued without blocking anything.**Ratification of the transaction slice's payload plumbing, recorded here rather than only in the hub message that authorised it.** S6's delegation named the family and its Ref twin plus the session dispatch's arm; it did not name `lean/Spec/SessionCodec.lean`, `lean/Ref/SessionCodec.lean` or the payload parameter added to `Session.step`. I authorised those mid-session over `hub` as the explicit planner update the ownership rule requires, and a reviewer was right that the authorisation then lived only in a transcript — the same gap this plan closed for an earlier slice's edits to `lean/Spec/Session.lean`, and the same remedy applies. The changes are ratified here: the two codecs and the `Session.step` signature, in both artefacts, symmetrically, with `Submission.frame` carrying the transfer's payload and no other caller affected — the reviewer confirmed `Session.step` has exactly two callers, both updated.

**And a Part 4 note for the dispositions pass, so it is not assumed covered.** `txn-declare.1` says other standard message sections SHOULD be ignored alongside a declare. Nothing enforces it and no vector exercises it either way: `bodyValue`, which the transaction dispatch calls, requires the decoded message to be exactly one `amqp-value` section, so a declare arriving with a header falls through as an ordinary opaque message rather than being recognised with its extra sections ignored. It is a SHOULD and not a violation, and the disposition must say it is unenforced rather than let a reader assume the MUST-class neighbours cover it.**Two corollaries of the two-artefact rule, learned from the slices working under it.** First, **a reference artefact is written without reading its specification twin**: the layer that produced `lean/Ref/Message.lean` spawned its writer deliberately so that it reads the artifacts and never `lean/Spec/Message.lean`, because a reference written against the specification it is compared with proves that two pieces of code share an author rather than that either matches the standard. In this repository the differential is the evidence, and this is what makes it evidence rather than agreement. Second, **a slice mutating a shared file announces the window**: which file, which mutants, one at a time, each built and reverted, and — the part that costs nothing and saves the most — that a build failing in that file during the window is the mutator's own plant and not a defect in anyone else's slice, with an announcement when the window closes. Both are cheap; both were worked out by the slices rather than by the plan.**The V-tiers were audited against the plan, and two of the four are outstanding** — checked in the repository rather than assumed from the plan's prose. V1 is partial: the codec's round trips and canonicality are proved and credit conservation is in flight as the S4 contract's law, but the session-level invariants the plan names — handle uniqueness, window conservation, delivery-number monotonicity — do not exist, **and `Spec/Session.lean`'s `handles` docstring says `Proofs/` holds the uniqueness statement**, which it does not. A promise in a docstring is a claim sourced from an intention, the same shape as the rest of this session's corrections, and it is either to be discharged or removed. V2 runs: the authored corpora, the frame corpus, the slice and now the sweep. V3 stays blocked and stated. **V4 — the specification-mutation controls — is entirely absent**: no mutant, no control and no record anywhere, and the plan makes it a slice acceptance item on the grounds that a corpus which cannot fail proves nothing. It is dispatched, together with the exit property the same acceptance list names and nothing checks: every state in the connection and session machines has a reachable mandated exit, which the sweep's model can decide because it already equals picture 30's ten edges.**A gate run before a commit validates the worktree, not the commit.** The census extension's 26 clauses were regenerated into `ledger/clauses.json` and the manifest was regenerated over the worktree, but only the dispositions were staged — so the commit carried the old `clauses.json` while the manifest recorded the new hash, and on a clean checkout `s0_spec_manifest.sh` would have failed, correctly. Nothing hid it: the gate was green when it ran and the tree was green when it ran, and the two were not the same tree. Stage everything a gate covers before trusting it over a commit, or re-run the gate after staging; the check is `git status` between the run and the commit, which is cheap and which nothing else performs.**The ledger was audited against the sweep's findings, and its existing claims survived.** Every disposition that touches a clause the sweep exercised — the flow and link statements deferred to S4, the channel-bound and channel-bound-error formalisations — states something still true, and none of the three findings falsifies one. Two structural facts came out of the audit and belong on the record, because neither is visible from the tooling's output alone:**Three rules about corpus shape, from the message slice's mutation window, and each one is a way a corpus fails to test what it appears to test.** First, **a rule has two sides and needs one vector per side**: the annotation-policy mutant was killed only because two vectors carry the *same octets* under different policies, so inverting the test turned an admission into a refusal and a refusal into an admission, and each vector's failure named the other's rule — one vector would have been green under the mutant and pinned nothing. Second, **a quantity has to be an observable or nothing tests it**: the omission mutant that dropped `rejected`'s delivery-count increment was caught by the count alone, because the state name is identical either way, which is why every quantity a clause moves must be reported rather than merely stored. Third, **a bound widened by one is visible only at exactly bound plus one** — the lesson the earlier V4 record already carries, and worth repeating because a vector comfortably past the bound passes under the mutant that widens it.* **The gate and the coverage report do different jobs.** Dispositions are *required* for keyword-free statements, reviewed pictures and ambiguity-register entries; keyword clauses are *counted* in the coverage report and reported as undispositioned (205 must-class clauses today) rather than gated. So the plan's phrase "every clause carries a disposition" is true of the gated classes and, for keyword clauses, is a running count that becomes complete as their milestones run. The state is visible rather than hidden, which is what the coverage report exists for — but a reader should not mistake a passing `check` for a ledger with no outstanding obligations.
* **The sweep's findings create three new ledger obligations**, each of which lands with its fix: the clause families whose refusals omit their condition (the unmapped-channel rule, the window-or-credit rule, and handle reuse) and the `sessions.4` statement, all of which currently sit inside that undispositioned count. When their vectors exist, their dispositions name the vector; until then the coverage report counts them, and nothing claims they are carried.**A citation can resolve and point at the wrong rule, and no gate here can see it.** When the collector's statement units change, the keyword-free statements under an anchor renumber, and a vector citing `.u1` keeps resolving while naming a different sentence — the citation gate checks that an id exists, the ledger check compares a disposition's pinned text against its clause, and neither knows what a *vector* meant to cite. It has happened twice in a day: `session-flow-control.4`'s disposition named the received-transfer constructor for a clause about a received flow, caught by hand while writing the window contract; and the transaction slice's two detach vectors cited `.u1` under the `transactions` anchor after the census's `is allowed` shape took that index for the new no-transactional-work sentence, caught because the new sentence's id happened to be quoted to that slice. The dispositions survive renumbering because they pin their text and the vectors do not, so the convention is a pass over the affected anchors whenever the collector's units move, with `git diff` of the ledger's `.u1`-shaped entries as the signal and the checker's stale list as the ledger-side one. No contract can do this without knowing intent, which is why it is a convention.**The session sweep ran, and its first finding is the argument for its existence.** 127 probes / 278 steps against both artefacts, every step answered by both, 183 agreements, 3 observations, **no mispredicted outcome and no scatter** — the model's expectation table held everywhere it was tested, including the mandator constants (a handle-reuse probe predicting `connection:CLOSE_SENT`, two `handle-max` probes predicting the same state with `framing-error` and class `limit`), and the unattached-handle family came out 21 cells refused with nothing mixed, confirming the flow sibling in both directions. That is the instrument behaving as an expectation table rather than as a recorder.**A contract comparing exchange corpora reads refusals per step, because `expectError` is the value corpora's whole-vector vocabulary.** The transaction contract was written by copying the frame corpora's collection, and the exchange corpora state a refusal on the step: `steps[i].expect.status == "refused"` with its condition and reason, at the id the runner emits (`vector#<index+1>`). Reading `expectError` left the refusal set empty, so the non-vacuity guard fired — and its message blamed the corpus, which was right, for not exercising refusals it exercised seven times. That is worse than a missed check: a guard that misdirects teaches a reader to distrust a working corpus, and the repair belongs in the contract rather than in the thing it accuses.**And then the finding the differential is structurally blind to**: **in 38 cells both artefacts refuse with the right class and name no protocol condition at all** — 34 where the channel map refuses a frame on a channel that is not the session's, 3 where a window or credit is exhausted, 1 where a handle is reused. The two layers *agree* in all 38, and no corpus vector pins a condition on those cells, so the differential reports nothing: **an omission shared by both implementations is invisible to every instrument that compares them to each other**, and only a model derived from the clauses can see it. In each case the class is exactly what the register's ruling says and only the condition is absent, which reads as the condition having been decided and then not carried through the refusal. Each of the three families needs a vector, since the pin is what would let the differential see it thereafter.**Amendment to §13's S5 acceptance text, recorded the way S2's narrowing was.** §13 named `lean/Contracts/MessageFormat.lean` and two acceptance criteria for the message layer — that every section type round-trips and that the outcome state machine is proved. Neither landed in this wave, and the delegation actually given ran five rungs: the section types, the codec in both artefacts, the outcome machine, the corpus, the differential. That was a deliberate re-scoping and not an oversight, so the record says so rather than leaving §13 to read as an unmet claim: the five rungs are what the wave carried, the contract and the round-trip proof are the milestone's remaining obligation, and they are planner-authored like every acceptance declaration here. S6's caveat is the same shape — its implementation, corpus and mutation controls are complete while the milestone is not closed — and both wait on the same piece of work. Also outstanding from the same review: `s1_proof_integrity.sh`'s per-theorem allowlist does not yet cover the message layer's seven theorems, so their axiom inventories are disclosed by the blanket trust scan and not by the per-theorem one.**Two more findings, both located.** In 54 cells the layers disagree: 28 where the reference says `amqp:illegal-state` and the specification still says `framing-error` — the 159-step correction this session made reached the reference and not the specification — 8 where the specification is on the clause's side and the reference says `framing-error` (`unattached-handle`, `handle-in-use`, `invalid-field`, the three rules added latest), and **20 in behaviour, where the reference refuses every frame it receives in `END_SENT` and every frame it sends in `END_RCVD`, while `sessions.10` and `sessions.11` permit exactly those, and the refusals' own words give the reason: the specification says "channel 1 is not mapped to this session, whose incoming channel is none", the reference says the same, and the artifact assigns `END_SENT` an incoming channel and `END_RCVD` an outgoing one** — so the reference's two states lack the channel maps the artifact gives them. And separately, `sessions.4` is unimplemented in both: our own begin setting `remote-channel` when locally initiated is refused (so `sessions.3` is enforced), but the *answer* to our begin leaving `remote-channel` empty is admitted, and **nothing in the corpus covers it**.**And what a `Conforms` instance cannot check, found by the first two.** `Conforms` is a simulation over a step relation: it relates states and outputs, which is why it can carry output agreement, the classes of refusals and the states a step leaves, and why the connection layer's instance covers all three parts of its observable. It cannot carry the obligations §10 states *beside* the relation — progress under a stated fairness assumption (rule 4) and the timing assumptions (rule 6) — because there is no step to simulate when the obligation is that a step eventually happens. The connection slice read the second one out of the code rather than assuming it: neither tree holds a timer, `Submission` has no tick constructor, and the field the artifact's timer clauses hang off is read nowhere, so the alphabet's tick channel gets no answer from either layer. That is the layer's actual behaviour and a scope statement rather than a defect, and it is now recorded in the `idle-time-out` disposition as well as here, because a proof's doc comment is where such a thing would be lost. **The first supervisor question is whether the milestone is the instances alone, or the instances plus these obligations, and in what order.****The sweep's own instrument was found defective three times by its own run, and reported rather than smoothed** — the attach role inverted (the artifact's choice is sender `false`, receiver `true`, against the conventional reading, so every probe claimed the opposite role; a sender's attach now carries `initial-delivery-count`, which MUST NOT be null for that role), probe transfers missing `delivery-tag` and `message-format`, which let the field rule answer three window cells before the window could, and probe flows carrying `next-incoming-id` unconditionally, where it is set exactly when the peer has begun. Two of the three are the isolation error again, in the probes rather than in the corpus.**If a proof has to compare it, it must be a term — and three findings in two layers say so.** The frame layer's instance found that a refusal's class was recovered from its rendered detail with `String.splitOn`, which no tactic can reason about, and made the class a field on both sides. The connection layer's instance then found the same shape twice more: the specification's connection refusal carries no class field at all, and the reference's connection layer recovers one by splitting the frame layer's prose on every frame-decode failure — proved unprovable rather than suspected, with the goal `(("truncated: " ++ text).splitOn ":").head? = some "truncated"` left unsolved and no `splitOn` lemmas in the pinned library to reach for. The pattern is one sentence: **a value recoverable only from rendered prose is invisible to the kernel, and a hypothesis resting on one is a permanent hole rather than an obligation** — it reads like a claim awaiting proof while being, in fact, undischargeable. Making the class a field is the cheaper repair in every case, and it is the one the instances took. The corollary matters as much as the rule: where the *rendered* string is what the corpus and the differential compare, the check for that is a line-by-line diff of every verdict detail before and after the change, because equality in fact is not equality in proof.****Two admissions are consequences of the single-link model rather than rules, and they belong on the record before a sweep probes them.** A second `attach` on a live link naming a *different* handle is admitted, because the session models one link's flow state while keeping a registry of handles; and a link whose *both* endpoints are sender is admitted, because no clause forbids it. Neither is a rule and neither is a defect: they are what a one-link session means, so a cell that admits them measures the model rather than the specification. Their complements, stated by the same implementer as facts, are the cells that *should* refuse — a duplicate `attach` handle (`handle-in-use`, with the connection moving to `CLOSE_SENT`), an `attach` outside the partner's announced `handle-max` (`framing-error`), a `flow`, `transfer` or `detach` naming an unattached handle (`unattached-handle`, with the `detach` exception admitted), a handle-less flow carrying `link-credit`, `delivery-count`, `available`, `drain` or `properties` (`invalid-field`, `echo` deliberately excluded), a transfer against either role's direction rules, and a delivery breaking `settled.4` under `sender-settle-mode`.**A send-direction vector's octets are not the octets that travel.** A send step is handed to the peer's own writer, which decodes and re-encodes it, so a body written in a non-canonical form leaves the vector's stated octets behind: the size-probe that was meant to pin a frame one octet over a declared maximum actually sent a frame twelve octets under it, because its three integers were written in the wide five-octet form and the writer emitted them canonically. The vector then passes for the right reason and witnesses nothing — a defect in the *probe* that reads exactly like the rule being unreachable, which is how it was written into a disposition note and queued as a lift. The rule for authoring one: a send-direction vector is a claim about what the writer produces, so its octets must be the writer's, and a probe about octets *on the wire* belongs on the receive side, where they arrive unaltered. And the corollary that cost a day: when a recorded defect stops being reproducible, the record is what is wrong — measurement against the current tree is the check, and a queued fix resting on an unmeasured defect is a change nobody justified.**And the known-state rule's boundary is sharper than the version this plan wrote**: a note may state what is *implemented* and what a *scope decision excludes* — both facts about the repository — but not what a future disagreement would *mean*, which pre-empts the report that has to carry the judgment. A catalogue of the cells one *expects to refuse* is the inverse of the error and is welcome, because it preserves a finding by saying in advance which disagreements are real rather than which are expected.**A proof is proved when the build says so, and a lemma under test belongs in the module whose API it extends.** A slice reported that an obligation was "proved in scratch" and then withdrew it: the scratch imported the module the obligation belongs to, which already declares that name, so every run died on a name clash rather than on the proof, and the claim had never been observed by a compiler. It found this itself, named the cause, restored the last building assembly, and reported the state as one obligation larger rather than smaller — which is the whole discipline in one paragraph, because a report that says *proved* about something no compiler saw is the failure mode this repository exists to catch, and catching it in one's own report costs a paragraph while leaving it costs the trust every other line rests on.**A revert is verified by re-running the check, not by assuming the copy back worked.** A slice working directly in a shared module keeps a revert guard around every attempt, and reports `after revert, error lines: 0` as the last line of a failed one — the guard restores the last building assembly and then *re-runs* the check, so a restore that silently failed cannot pass for a clean tree. On a shared tree this is not politeness: a sibling's slice is in the same repository, so an unverified revert would leave someone else's work building against a half-applied edit of yours, and the failure would surface in their turn rather than yours. Two attempts, both reverted, both verified, and the tree never left the state its report described. That is the mutation-window discipline — announce, hold, revert, verify — applied to edits that are not mutants.The rule it produced: **a scratch file cannot test a name the module declares**, because it inherits that namespace by importing it — the same collision that bit the second instance's generic names an hour earlier. So a lemma under test is written *in* the module, and the build is the test. That is not a fallback: the build is the only instrument that has seen any of the six defects the proof obligations produced, and a scratch was never an instrument at all.**Definitional equality is not syntactic equality, and neither is a proof question.** Two layers' header predicates are provably the same function — equal by `rfl`, checked by the tables slice — but they unfold to different spellings, `≥` on one side and `≤` on the other, so a tactic that unfolds both rewrites into two forms `simp` cannot see are equal and fails with a type mismatch that reads like a wrong hypothesis. The repair is not to find the right lemma; it is to write the term with **no tactic at all**, because the types are definitionally equal and Lean accepts it directly. And when a `simp` collapses a conjunction of equalities to `True`, the constructor changes with it — `⟨trivial, …⟩` rather than `⟨rfl, …⟩`, with `expected to have type True` as the tell. Both cost a slice four attempts and were diagnosed by reproducing them in a single-file test *first*, then bringing the fixed file back verbatim so the landing was one line. The general rule: **when a tactic's failure does not match the goal it was given, suspect the spelling and not the mathematics** — and the order that works is the small tested piece first, the assembly second.**One `Proofs` namespace, not one per layer, so an instance names its endpoints and its relation per layer.** The second instance to be written could not reuse the first's generic names — `specStep`, `refStep`, `specConnection`, `refConnection` and `R` were already declared in `SpecAMQP.Proofs`, and the module failed to compile until each became layer-specific. Neither proof was wrong; the namespace simply has room for one instance's generics. The convention is cheap now and expensive later: a reader moving between `Proofs.FrameConformance` and `Proofs.ConnectionConformance` should not have to work out which `R` is which, and an acceptance module has to name each instance's relation explicitly.

**Two things the first instance to use the frame channel had to discover, both now stated so the next does not.** The alphabet's `Input.frame` and `Output.frame` carry `ByteArray` while the specification's wire is `Harness.Octets`, which is `Array UInt8` — different types, with no conversion named anywhere in §10. The conversion is `ByteArray.mk`/`ByteArray.data`, which are `rfl`-inverses, so nothing is copied and no fact about the octets is weakened; it is named here rather than left to be rediscovered because the frame layer's own answers all travel on the `api` channel and the connection layer is the first subject whose observable is the wire itself.**A refinement proof cannot catch a mutation in the vocabulary it defines, and that is a boundary of the method rather than a gap in it.** The connection instance's mutation record has three kills and two survivors, and the second survivor is structural: weakening the *output vocabulary* — dropping the reason class from the refusal answer — survives **by construction**, because the answer is built by a function both endpoints share, so removing an observable removes it from both sides of the comparison and the simulation still holds. The observables are not justified by the proof; they are justified by the **contract** — `checkExchangeStep` compares exactly the condition, the reason class and the state, and §10 rule 5 makes the mandated error code part of the observable. So the division of labour is exact: **a mutation the proof can catch is one in a layer or in the relation; a mutation in the vocabulary the proof defines is caught by the corpus and by review of the vocabulary**, which is why the harness's comparison and the vector schema are contract files rather than implementation details. The other survivor is the honest kind — `R` weakened to the state table's state alone survived only because the fields it drops are read in the four arms still owed as `sorry`, and the slice predicts it must fail when they land, with the re-run promised either way. A survivor with a stated reason and a prediction is evidence; a survivor written down as equivalent is an assumption.And a behaviour the same instance measured rather than assumed: **a frame with no body is admitted writing nothing, even on the send side**, in both connection layers — the specification returns the endpoint unchanged and no octets, and the reference does the same — while the corpus codec reports the octets the *caller offered* as written. The two layers agree with each other, so this is not a divergence between artefacts; the codec's report is the odd one out, and it matters because a vector pinning what a bodyless send wrote would pin the codec's answer rather than the layer's. Recorded rather than repaired: the harness's report of what a step wrote is a design question about the corpus, and the honest place to decide it is beside the other harness semantics rather than in a proof's passing remark.**Fact 1 is done, and the composition turned out not to be additive.** The reader's progress is one package of six facts by induction on fuel — `readValue`, `readItems`, `readCompound`, `readArrayData`, `readElements`, `readElementsLoop` — proved in dependency order, with the array loop needing a second induction on its count because its recursive call keeps the fuel and decreases the count. What is worth keeping beyond the result: **the advance form is not a one-line composition of the measurement.** `c'.pos - start = size` together with the whole read's progress does *not* give `c'.pos = start + size`, because the subtraction truncates — a cursor past the end with size zero satisfies both — so the compound and array advances re-invert the read to get the *tail's* progress. That is the truncating subtraction again, and it is now the second place in this specification where a difference that has to be a sum comes from an arithmetic that saturates: the credit law's step case survives for the same reason, the gate making the truncation agree rather than wrap.**A false statement is evidence, and the first question is whether the model is wrong rather than whether the statement is too strong.** Six artefact defects in two layers were found by writing an obligation down and attempting it — the refusal class recovered from prose on each side, the condition divergence between two layers for one input, a SASL dialogue restarted by a second header exchange, an arm of the specification's SASL step that discards the octets its four sibling arms write, and a reference that resolves a descriptor's code form but not its declared-name form — and in every case the statement came out false, the falsehood was a defect, and the slice could have weakened its statement instead of reporting. That last shape is the failure this rule exists to forbid: **a statement trimmed to fit a bug turns the proof from a test into a transcript**, and it is indistinguishable afterwards from a statement that was right all along. So the order is: write the obligation, attempt it, and when it fails ask which side is wrong — and the answer has been the model four times out of four. The corollary is about instruments: two of those four defects were invisible to the corpus, which reads what a *caller offered* rather than what a layer *wrote*, so a green capture proves no regression and never proves coverage. Say which instrument saw a fix, and say when none could. The six split two ways: four were invisible to the corpus as well as to the kernel — the condition divergence for one input, the SASL dialogue restart, the dropped write on an outbound outcome, and the reference resolving a descriptor's code form only — and each of those was found by the proof and by nothing else. The other two, the refusal class recovered from prose on each side, were *visible* to the corpus, which compares classes, and invisible to the *kernel*, which cannot split a rendered string. Neither axis subsumes the other, and that is the point of having both.**Fact 2 is done except for its value-dependent comparison, and my instruction for it was wrong in a way worth recording.** I asked for the table fact by the same method as the variable family's — `decide` over the generated encodings. That method applies where the needed fact is a property *of the table*; the fixed family needs a relationship *between two lookups*, and the two decided readings both fail this repository's standard: "the row found at a width has that width" is `List.find?`'s filtering, **a fact that cannot fail**, and a per-owner width inventory transcribes the table the decide is supposed to read. What landed instead is the content actually needed — the writer selects a row by owner and width, the reader is handed a row of the same table, and any row the lookup returns declares the width it asked for — which is soundness of the lookup rather than an inventory of it. **The method's boundary is therefore narrower than my instruction implied, and the deviation is the correction**: decide over the table where the fact is about the table, state the lookup's soundness where the fact is about a relationship. What remains for the fixed family is its canonical *comparison*, which is per-owner and value-dependent — the writer picks a width from the value, uint being 0, 1 or 4 — and is named in the proof rather than written.

**Fact 3's "not small" is established rather than asserted.** Three probes: `#check` on the plausible core names (`String.fromUTF8?_toUTF8`, `String.toUTF8_fromUTF8?`, `String.fromUTF8?_eq_some`) all report unknown identifier; a grep of mathlib's sources finds no `fromUTF8` lemma at all; and `exact?` on the exact goal `String.fromUTF8? s.toUTF8 = some s` at four hundred thousand heartbeats closes nothing. So the one blocker that is not work is now a measured absence rather than a suspicion, and the law's statement is untouched by any of it.

**And the ladder's next step makes the remaining distance a hypothesis rather than an absence.** What is left for the narrowest-form law is the per-family canonical *size comparisons* — the step that turns each family's arithmetic into the inequality the law states — and the right shape for the assembly is not to attempt it and fail, because a Lean proof has no way to leave a hole without `sorry`. It is to write the composition with the two text families' comparisons as **explicit hypotheses**, so the repository holds the law's full logical structure with the one blocker visible as a named premise: when the UTF-8 lemma arrives, the law's proof is two instantiations away rather than a sitting of work. The comparisons that do not need it can then be proved outright — `binary` is clear of the gap, the compounds, arrays and described families have their arithmetic in hand, and the fixed family's is the per-owner value-dependent one, the writer picking a width from the value. That is the last *work* on this ladder; everything else on it is either done or waiting on a fact the standard library does not have.

**The three facts between the law's statement and its proof, in the order they should be attempted:**

1. **The reader never moves its cursor backwards** — a mutual induction over the reader's recursive cluster (`readValue`, `readCompound`, `readArrayData`, `readItems`, `readElements`, `readElementsLoop`). The compound and array reading-off steps report the size as the reader *measured* it (`c'.pos - start = size`), and converting that into an advance (`c'.pos = start + size`) needs `start ≤ c'.pos`. `omega` refuses the conversion without it, and the refusal was verified rather than assumed. Statable and provable here: work, not a gap.
2. **The fixed family's reading-off step**, whose canonical comparison also needs the writer's table lookup to agree with the reader's row — a table fact of the same kind as `variable_row_width`.
3. **The UTF-8 lemma** — octets that decode to a text re-encode to those octets. Core states nothing about `String.fromUTF8?` or `String.toUTF8` beyond `toUTF8_eq_toByteArray`, batteries states nothing, and this is **the one blocker that is not work**. It is what would stop the text families' size comparison and hence the law as stated, so it is the single most valuable upstream contribution this programme can name.

**A repository hazard worth a rule, found while proving the fixed family.** Lean's `autoImplicit` silently turns an unknown identifier into an implicit variable, so a first draft using `Category`, `EncodingDecl` or `encodingOf` outside their scope *type-checks as nonsense* — it compiles, and the statement means something other than what was written. That belongs to the family above in its most dangerous form, because the check that should catch it is the type-checker and the type-checker is what silently agrees. The repository's discipline for proof files therefore includes opening the generated surface explicitly rather than relying on resolution, and two further Lean-level lessons are recorded in the proof file's own documentation where the next prover will meet them: `cases hb : e` inside a goal that mentions `e` rewrites the goal occurrence and leaves `hb` unusable exactly where the conclusion needs it (invert from an existential goal instead), and splitting a match needs `split at h` rather than `cases` on the discriminant.

**The traps, collected in one place because every rung has paid for at least one.** The closure has no Mathlib, so a Mathlib spelling of a lemma is a name to avoid rather than a thing to import (`Nat.pow_succ`, not `pow_succ`; `by_cases`, not `by_contra`), and `norm_num` is unavailable. `prefix` is a Lean keyword and cannot be a binder name. `Array.foldl_toList` is oriented list-ward, so the Array-to-List bridge is `← Array.foldl_toList`, and `Functor.map` on `Except.ok` does not reduce under `simp`, so the proof crosses it with an explicit `show`. And the one that has now recurred across R2, R3 and R4 and is therefore a property of this codebase rather than of any rung: **every arithmetic goal over the writer's output wants `simp [List.length_append, beOctets_length] <;> omega`** — associativity and commutativity of `+` are never in `simp`'s normal form for the goal shapes the writer's length expressions produce, so `simp` alone leaves `head.length + tail.length + 1 = 1 + head.length + tail.length` open.
